however, López Obrador criticized the newly elected Supreme Court president and his administration sought to weaken existing safeguards, while proposed cybersecurity penalties threatened broad online expression.", "question_ids": ["3B.8", "4B.3", "4B.4", "4B.6", "4B.9", "7B.6", "7B.7"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "Directly covers the 2023 country-report period, with some cited judicial decisions from 2022.", "ruler_attribution": "The report attributes criticism and policy proposals to López Obrador and attributes protective rulings to the independent judiciary.", "limitations": ["The passage combines constitutional guarantees, judicial decisions, and executive proposals; these should not be treated as equivalent forms of implementation.", "The report does not show whether all proposed criminal provisions were enacted."], "start_unit": 41, "end_unit": 45, "locator": "extracted HTML block 41 through extracted HTML block 45", "exact_excerpt": "Violations of User Rights\nC1\n1.00-6.00 pts\n0-6 pts\nDo the constitution or other laws fail to protect rights such as freedom of expression, access to information, and press freedom, including on the internet, and are they enforced by a judiciary that lacks independence?\n3.00\n3\n6.00\n6\nThe constitution and its regulatory laws guarantee freedom of speech, freedom of the press, privacy of personal communications, and freedom of access to information. A constitutional reform in 2013 established internet access as a human right and guaranteed net neutrality (see B6). However, the López Obrador administration’s policies and proposals have sought to undermine existing safeguards for these rights (see A5, B3, C2 and C6).\nThe judiciary is currently regarded as generally independent. Following the election of Norma Lucía Piña Hernández as the first woman president of the SCJN in January 2023, President López Obrador has voiced dissatisfaction with her appointment and the recent role of the judiciary,\n1\nand supporters of López Obrador have targeted Piña with an online disinformation campaign (see B5). The former president of the SCJN, Arturo Zaldívar, had been viewed as a López Obrador ally.\nDespite concerns about diminished autonomy under López Obrador in recent years, the judiciary has exhibited impartiality and ruled in favor of human rights online, as with the SCJN’s decision against the “right to be forgotten” in November 2022 and its invalidation of a biometric cell phone registry in April 2022 (see B3 and C4). In the latter ruling, Zaldívar emphasized the lack of security in data handling and the consequent lack of justification for the state to maintain so much private information, as well as the registry’s likely ineffectiveness in combating crime.\n2\n1\nTras designación de la ministra Norma Piña en la Corte aumentaron actos ilegales e injustos, acusa AMLO [After Minister Norma Piña's appointment to the Court, illegal and unjust acts increased, accuses AMLO],” El Universal, January 20, 2023,\nhttps://www.eluniversal.com.mx/nacion/amlo-tras-designacion-de-la-minis…\n; AMLO acusa a la ministra Norma Piña y al CJF de no informar sobre jueces que liberan a delincuentes [AMLO accuses Minister Norma Piña and CJF of failing to report on judges who free criminals],” El Universal, February 24, 2023,\nhttps://www.eluniversal.com.mx/nacion/amlo-acusa-la-ministra-norma-pina…\n2\nLuis Fernando García, @tumbolian. “Para el @ArturoZaldivarL los datos biométricos solo pueden ser obtenidos por el Estado en casos excepcionales. También coincide con el proyecto. (Muy relevante). 4-0 faltan 4 para que muera el #PANAUT definitivamente.”, Twitter, April 25, 2022.\nhttps://twitter.com/tumbolian/status/1518646492038410241\n; Darinka Rodriguez, “La Suprema Corte de México invalida la creación de un padrón de datos biométricos para usuarios de celulares,” El País, April 25, 2022,\nhttps://elpais.com/mexico/2022-04-25/la-suprema-corte-de-mexico-invalid…\n\nC2\n1.00-4.00 pts\n0-4 pts\nAre there laws that assign criminal penalties or civil liability for online activities, particularly those that are protected under international human rights standards?\n2.00\n2\n4.00\n4\nProvisions from both the criminal and civil codes continue to be used to intimidate ordinary users and journalists, including those who publish online. For example, Article 277 of the state penal code in San Luis Potosí criminalizes those who insult authority with one-to-three-year prison sentences.\n1\nAlthough defamation was decriminalized at the federal level in 2007, state-level criminal defamation statutes persist.\n2\nIn June 2022, deputy Rafael Alejandro Micalco Méndez presented a bill in the Puebla legislature that would recriminalize defamation and slander in the state’s penal code.\n3\nA group of civil society organizations, including the Puebla Journalists Network and the Committee to Protect Journalists (CPJ), warned of the potential chilling effect the bill could have on freedom of expression and journalistic work, particularly for investigations related to acts of corruption, organized crime, and serious human rights violations.\n4\nThe proposal faced opposition in the Puebla legislature and was not adopted during the coverage period.\n5\nEfforts to remove the crime of defamation from Yucatán’s state penal code stalled during the coverage period. In February 2022, a group of journalists spearheaded an initiative to decriminalize defamation in the state, citing concerns over freedom of expression.\n6\nHowever, despite continued efforts by the journalistic coalition, the legislature had not yet decriminalized defamation as of November 2022.\n7\nLegislation to criminalize hate speech, discrimination, terrorism, and misinformation at the federal and state levels has been proposed in recent years, though no proposals were passed by the end of the coverage period.\n8\nThe proposed Federal Cybersecurity Law (see B3 and C5), presented in the Chamber of Deputies in April 2023, introduces criminal penalties for overly broad forms of online expression. For instance, Article 78 of the bill broadly criminalizes online expressions that “incite or consist of terrorism, or advocate national, racial, sexual or religious hatred, or constitute discrimination,” in addition to actions that “systematically, automatically and intentionally misinform the population causing the individual or collective manipulation of people.”\n\n9\nCriminal sanctions range from three to six years in prison, and fines of approximately $2,700 to $5,400 as of 2023.\nThe movement to criminalize the nonconsensual sharing of intimate images saw major developments in recent years. In April 2021, Congress approved reforms to the General Law on Women's Access to a Life Free of Violence and the federal criminal code, legislation known as the national Olympia Law, which criminalizes the nonconsensual sharing of intimate images and punishes “digital violence.”\n10\nIt includes provisions for three-to-six-year prison sentences and fines of up to 90,000 pesos ($4,669) for those found guilty of digital violence as defined by the law.\n11\nAs of December 2021, all of the country’s 32 states had passed laws in line with the federal Olympia Law.\n12\nGender and digital rights organizations criticized a number of the state laws for being disproportionate, potentially prompting censorship, and lacking reparations for victims.\n13\nThe Senate approved the federal law in November 2020, without adjusting the law to incorporate criticisms raised by civil society groups, including the ambiguity of concepts like “digital violence” and the vagueness of what constitutes “intimate sexual content.”\n14\nNonetheless, the Olympia Law did not appear to have been used to censor journalists as of the end of the coverage period.\n1\nJuan Vázquez, “Funcionarios de San Luis on LinkedIn (Opens in new window)\nLinkedIn\nShare on X (Opens in new window)\nX\nShare on WhatsApp (Opens in new window)\nWhatsApp\nEmail a link to a friend (Opens in new window)\nEmail\nMexico City, May 11, 2023—Mexican President Andrés Manuel López Obrador must stop making baseless criticisms of local news outlets and the international free expression organization Article 19, the Committee to Protect Journalists said Thursday.\nSince March, López Obrador has sharply criticized Article 19, national investigative magazine Proceso, privately owned online news outlets Animal Político and Aristegui Noticias, and Animal Político investigative reporter Nayeli Roldán over their coverage of the\nMexican federal government’s alleged use of illegal spyware\n.\nThe president’s statements have led to online abuse and threats of violence against Article 19, the three outlets, and their reporters, according to Roldán, Animal Político’s editorial director Daniel Moreno, and Article 19’s regional director Leopoldo Maldonado, who all spoke to CPJ by phone.\n“Mexican President López Obrador’s recent attempts to discredit journalist Nayeli Roldán, three critical news outlets, and Article 19 are more proof that his administration prefers harassing journalists over solving the country’s catastrophic press freedom crisis,” said CPJ Mexico Representative Jan-Albert Hootsen. “López Obrador’s constant verbal attacks on reporters, which serve only as a distraction from the issues they report on, must stop before they lead to further violence against the press.”\nSince he assumed office in 2018, López Obrador\nrepeatedly stated\nthat his government\ndoes not engage in illegal surveillance with spyware\nand denied that his administration uses such applications for\nanything other than national security\n.\n\nHowever,\na series\nof reports\npublished in March 2023 provided evidence that the Mexican military used Pegasus, a spyware developed by the Israeli NSO group, to monitor conversations between human rights activist Raymundo Ramos and two journalists at the Mexico City newspaper El Universal since 2019.\nIn a\nMarch 10 press briefing\n, Roldán asked López Obrador about those allegations, to which he responded by saying Roldán was “always against his government.” When Roldán insisted the military must explain the legal basis for the spying, he accused her of “not being objective,” and called her “unprofessional” and part of the “tendentious, bribed media.”\nDuring an April 28\npress conference\n, the president told reporters that Roldán was paid in 2022 by the National Institute for Access to Information, a federal autonomous body that handles freedom of information requests and regulates the protection of personal data. López Obrador has been highly critical of the institute, which he claims is “useless,” “onerous, opaque, and unnecessarily expensive,” and opposes his administration and him personally, according to\nnews\nreports\n.\nDuring a May 2\npress briefing\n, López Obrador accused Article 19 of being funded by the U.S. government to work “against his government,” therefore “violating our sovereignty” and called the organization “interventionist,” adding that he would send a diplomatic cable to the U.S. government “in protest.”\nMoreno, Roldán, and Maldonado told CPJ that the president’s remarks have led to many hateful comments on social media against them personally, as well as on websites and social media pages of Article 19, Proceso, Animal Político, and Aristegui Noticias. Roldán said she received “vicious” misogynistic comments, while Maldonado said he and his organization received many threats and statements echoing the president’s comments.\n“I’ve been receiving lots of insults, an increasing number. I’d even call it stalking,” Roldán told CPJ, adding that the pressure has forced her to keep a lower profile on social media. “I can’t send out a single tweet without it receiving insults.”\nMoreno said the president’s comments have made him and his reporters feel less safe, leading some of his reporters to ask not to be named in bylines.\n“We try to respond to the president, who constantly lies about us and never rectifies false information. His daily press briefing is a far bigger platform than anything we could ever hope to have,” Moreno said. “We have seen an increase in the number of attacks and insults against us, including social media users openly asking who our family members are to accost them as well.”\nCPJ contacted presidential spokesperson Jesús Ramírez Cuevas for comment via messaging app but did not receive any response.\nMexico was the deadliest country in the Western Hemisphere for journalists in 2022. realizaba\nsobre la ejecución extrajudicial de tres jóvenes\n, presuntamente cometida por militares el 3 de julio de 2020 en Nuevo Laredo, Tamaulipas.\nAsí se advierte en el reporte de inteligencia número 2001-1661, localizado entre los millones de archivos de la Sedena que fueron hackeados\npor el grupo Guacamaya\nen octubre pasado.\nMientras, Citizen Lab, de la Universidad de Toronto, confirmó que la infección del teléfono de Ramos con Pegasus ocurrió en agosto de 2020, periodo que coincide con las publicaciones del diario\nEl Universal\nque revelaron el asesinato de los jóvenes, desmintiendo la versión oficial de la Sedena respecto de que lo ocurrido en Tamaulipas había sido un enfrentamiento que dejó “12 agresores reducidos”.\nA esto se suma que, en diciembre de 2022, Ramos recibió una notificación de la empresa Apple sobre ataques a su teléfono con la siguiente alerta: “Apple cree que estás siendo objetivo de atacantes patrocinados por el Estado que intentan comprometer tu equipo remotamente a través de tu cuenta de Apple”.\n\nRaymundo Ramos, presidente del Comité de Derechos Humanos de Nuevo Laredo, ha acompañado casos de violaciones a derechos humanos desde hace 20 años, sobre todo a raíz de la llamada\n“guerra contra el narco”\nemprendida por Felipe Calderón, cuando las Fuerzas Armadas comenzaron a hacer labores de seguridad pública que derivaron en el uso excesivo de la fuerza y ejecuciones. La más reciente situación ocurrió el 26 de febrero pasado,\ncuando siete jóvenes fueron atacados por militares y cinco de ellos murieron\n;\nRamos también acompaña este caso\n.\nEl Ejército espía\nEl reporte del espionaje de 2020 fue elaborado por el Centro Militar de Inteligencia, una estructura secreta dentro del Ejército que —según documentos internos— obtiene información a través de\n“medios cerrados”\n, y tiene personal especializado e infraestructura tecnológica dedicada a “procesar y obtener información sensible sobre los temas que sean del interés del Alto Mando”, es decir, del secretario de la Defensa.\nFOTO: Especial\nEl CMI es, además, el “usuario final”, del servicio del “monitoreo remoto” contratado por la Sedena\na la empresa Comercializadora Antsua\n, la única proveedora\ndel software Pegasus\nen México, como se advierte en el oficio número SGE-3335 de la Sedena, donde se informa sobre las facturas de pago.\nLa información sobre el CMI y el reporte de inteligencia revelados en esta investigación, realizada por la Red en Defensa de los Derechos Digitales (R3D), Artículo 19, SocialTic,\nAnimal Político\n,\nAristegui Noticias\ny\nProceso\n, fueron obtenidos entre los millones de archivos de la Sedena hackeados por el grupo Guacamaya en octubre pasado.\nEl reporte de inteligencia tiene como asunto: “Informar las actividades de Jesús Raymundo Ramos Vázquez, presidente del Comité de Derechos Humanos de Nuevo Laredo A. C. para desprestigiar a las Fuerzas Armadas con fines de lucro y en beneficio del Cártel del Noreste”.\n\nAunque Ramos ha acompañado casos de víctimas de violaciones de derechos humanos y en ese momento asesoraba a las familias de los jóvenes asesinados en Nuevo Laredo en 2020,\nel Ejército intentaba criminalizarlo\n, pues en el reporte asentó que “Ramos Vázquez mantuvo una relación de amistad con Ana Isabel Treviño Morales, ‘La señora’, hermana de Miguel Ángel Treviño Morales, ‘Z-40’, y Óscar Omar Treviño Morales, ‘Z-42’”.\nEn tanto, en la sección de “información”, los mandos reportaron el contenido de seis conversaciones por aplicación de mensajería y llamadas telefónicas que Ramos tuvo con tres periodistas entre julio y agosto de 2020, y en las que hablaron sobre la ejecución extrajudicial ocurrida días antes en Nuevo Laredo.\nEl documento remata con el apartado “recomendación”, en la que sugiere que “este producto de inteligencia se proporcione con carácter de confidencial a la Policía Ministerial Militar para que sirva como elemento de juicio para su investigación, sin agregarse a la carpeta de investigación”, según se lee en reporte elaborado por el entonces jefe del Estado Mayor de la Defensa Nacional, Homero Mendoza Ruiz.\nSe trata de\nla última pieza que confirma el espionaje\nen el sexenio de Andrés Manuel López Obrador perpetrado por el Ejército en contra de un defensor de derechos humanos, pese a que la Sedena no tiene facultad legal alguna para hacerlo. Durante los primeros seis meses del año, esta organización registró 272 agresiones. Esto equivale a una agresión cada 16 horas cometida este semestre en contra de periodistas y medios de comunicación.\nLas agresiones con mayor registro fueron las intimidaciones y hostigamientos con 68 casos (25% del total), seguidos del uso ilegítimo del poder público con 61 casos (22.43%), y amenazas, con 49 casos (18.01%).\nEntre los hechos de violencia que se registraron, se encuentran los asesinatos perpetrados en posible vínculo con la libertad de expresión de al menos 3 periodistas: Marco Aurelio Ramirez Hernandez el 23 de mayo, Luis Martín Sánchez Íñiguez el 7 de julio, y Nelson Matus Peña el 15 de julio. También se documentó la desaparición de Juan Carlos Hinojosa, el 6 de julio.\nEl Estado sigue siendo el principal agresor contra la prensa en México. Durante estos primeros seis meses del 2023, las autoridades fueron responsables de perpetrar 140 agresiones, es decir 1 de cada 2 ataques contra periodistas y medios. De los tres niveles de gobierno, las personas servidoras públicas a nivel estatal son quienes más atacaron a la prensa con el 23.5%, seguido por el nivel federal con 19.4% y en tercer lugar, a nivel municipal con 8.4%. Este periodo es la primera vez desde el año 2016 que el nivel federal corresponde al segundo lugar en agresiones, siendo comúnmente el último lugar.\nLas entidades con más agresiones documentadas por ARTICLE 19 contra la prensa fueron la Ciudad de México (65 casos, o 23.90% del total), Quintana Roo (21 casos, equivalente a 7.72%), Guanajuato (19, es decir el 6.99%), Puebla (18, o el 6.62%), y Coahuila (17, o el 6.25%).\nAl final del informe, presentamos una serie de recomendaciones a los poderes ejecutivos federal y estatales, a los poderes legislativos federal y estatales, a la Fiscalía General de la República y las instancias de Procuración de justicias, al Mecanismo de Protección de Personas Defensoras y Periodistas, y a la Comisión Ejecutiva Federal y las Comisiones Estatales de Víctimas.\nNota para prensa\nPara más información, favor de escribir a comunicacion@article19.org\nARTICLE 19 es una organización independiente de Derechos Humanos que trabaja alrededor del mundo para proteger y promover el derecho a la libertad de expresión. EN\nMexico\nMedia landscape\nMexico is one of the countries with the highest media concentrations in the world, and it is difficult for small, independent entities to carve out a place for themselves. The telecom sector is dominated by Telmex, while broadcasting is controlled by\nTelevisa\n. And the newspaper sector is essentially led by Organización Editorial Mexicana, which owns 70 Mexican daily newspapers, 24 radio stations and 44 websites. This is why more and more independent journalists post their work on social media.\n\nPolitical context\nPresident López Obrador and other government officials have adopted a combative and stigmatising rhetoric towards the press, frequently accusing journalists of supporting the opposition. Every Wednesday, the government runs a session called, \"Who's Who in this Week’s Fake News,” in yet another attempt to discredit journalists. During his four years in office, López Obrador has criticised journalists for their lack of professionalism, and has described the Mexican press as “biased”, “unfair” and \"the scum of journalism\".\nLegal framework\nPress freedom is guaranteed in the Mexican Constitution and further upheld in the Law Regarding Freedom of the Press of 1917. In practice, there are no overtly restrictive laws that curtail or censor press freedom, with censorship imposed with threats or direct attacks against the journalists rather than lawsuits, imprisonment, or rulings of suspension of activities.\nEconomic context\nMexico's economy is diversified and relies on a variety of sectors, including the hi-tech industry, oil production, mineral exploitation and manufacturing. Although it is the second largest economy in Latin America after Brazil, Mexico was hard-hit by the pandemic, with the loss of more than 2 million jobs. For the most part, the media have also been badly hit and journalists have had to seek alternative sources of income while attempting to continue their freelance work.\nSociocultural context\nMexico is a huge country that is structured around major population centres including Mexico City and at least seven other cities with more than a million inhabitants. The popularity of\ntelenovelas\nhas traditionally been the link between mass media and the sources of news and information for the general population.\nTV Azteca\nand\nTelevisa\n, TV stations owned by families close to the government, are by far the biggest suppliers of both.\nSafety\nCollusion between officials and organized crime poses a grave threat to journalists’ safety and cripples the judicial system at all levels. Journalists who cover sensitive political stories or crime, especially at the local level, are warned, threatened and then often gunned down in cold blood. Others are abducted and never seen again, or they flee to other parts of the country or abroad as the only way to ensure their survival. President López Obrador has not carried out the reforms needed to rein in this violence and impunity. Nearly 150 journalists have been murdered in Mexico since 2000 and 28 have gone missing.\nSupport us\nYour donations enable RSF to keep working. We depend on you in order to be able to monitor respect for press freedom and take action worldwide.\nMake a donation\nBecome a member\nMedia freedom is a fundamental right, but nearly half of the world’s population has no access to freely reported news and information.\nWe need you. Los aspectos más destacados incluyen: […]\nMéxico\nEl Comité tomó nota de que el Registro Nacional de Personas Desaparecidas y No Localizadas había registrado 111,540 personas como desaparecidas en México desde el 1 de enero de 1962 al 12 de septiembre de 2023, y expresó preocupación porque los datos se están actualizando sin seguir el Protocolo Homologado de Búsqueda y los estándares internacionales. El Comité llamó a México a asegurar la transparencia de la metodología utilizada para actualizar el Registro Nacional a fin de garantizar la confiabilidad de los datos recopilados, y que el proceso sea coordinado por la Comisión Nacional de Búsqueda, de manera independiente e imparcial. El Comité también subrayó que México debe establecer mecanismos que aseguren la participación de los colectivos de víctimas y adoptar medidas para evitar su revictimización y proteger la seguridad de la información contenida en el Registro.\nEl Comité expresó su preocupación por la investigación inadecuada del alarmante número de desapariciones, con su consecuente bajo número de condenas y una impunidad casi absoluta. Instó al Estado parte a garantizar la investigación inmediata, imparcial y exhaustiva de las denuncias de desapariciones forzadas, pero también de las desapariciones cometidas por personas o grupos que actúan sin la autorización, el apoyo o la aquiescencia del Estado. Convención para ayudar a los Estados a convertirse en partes de la Convención y responder a las preguntas comúnmente planteadas por los Estados cuando consideran este compromiso, con una caja de herramientas para alentar y apoyar a los Estados a unirse para prevenir y erradicar las desapariciones forzadas y luchar contra la impunidad de este atroz crimen.\nLas conclusiones del examen de país But Facts Show it is a Failed Strategy", "publisher": "Washington Office on Latin America", "raw_sha256": "4e2baf658dc6f9e7f0e4ff1edcdb9bcd8404a96bcf46d3eb165d5477a2809be0", "fact_summary": "WOLA reported that López Obrador moved from campaign promises to return the army to its barracks toward expanding and making military public-security deployment practically permanent, placing the National Guard under Sedena operational control and proposing legislative changes that WOLA said went against the Constitution's civilian designation. WOLA also reported reduced absolute levels of some serious human-rights violations and fewer armed confrontations, but continuing excessive-force and arbitrary-deprivation-of-life cases, weak accountability, refusal to comply with an Inter-American Court oversight ruling, and insufficient attention to strengthening civilian institutions.", "question_ids": ["2B.2", "2B.4", "3B.1", "3B.10", "3B.2", "3B.4", "3B.6", "3B.9", "4B.4", "4B.8", "8B.10", "8B.2", "8B.5", "8B.6", "8B.7", "8B.8", "8B.9"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "Published in September 2022; it describes policy direction and outcomes during López Obrador's administration that continued into the 2023 target period.", "ruler_attribution": "WOLA directly attributes the National Guard proposal, military-deployment policy, and stated instructions to López Obrador.", "limitations": ["WOLA's assessment is an expert interview and advocacy analysis rather than a court finding.", "The source gives limited quantitative evidence for 2023 and does not establish personal responsibility for individual abuses."], "start_unit": 3, "end_unit": 5, "locator": "extracted HTML block 3 through extracted HTML block 5", "exact_excerpt": "Contact\nOur Impact\nMultimedia\nVideo\nPodcast\nPodcast: Democracy&\nArchive\nSpanish\nWOLA\n›\nAnalysis\n›\nQ&A\n›\nMexico Deepens Militarization. But Facts Show it is a Failed Strategy\nQ&A\n2 Sep 2022\nMexico Deepens Militarization. But Facts Show it is a Failed Strategy\nMexico\nPeace & Security\n,\nRule of Law\nTeam WOLA\nContrary to his campaign promises to “return the army to its barracks,” Mexican President Andrés Manuel López Obrador has announced his intention to reform the National Guard so that it is “completely under” the authority of the Ministry of Defense (Sedena). Thus, the current government threatens to continue deepening the militarization of public security despite the high human rights costs of this strategy and its proven failure to address violence and drug trafficking, two major challenges in the country.\nWe spoke with WOLA’s Director for Mexico Stephanie Brewer about the present, past, and potential future of militarization as a security strategy in the country and the state of bilateral relations with the United States.\n\n1. What is the López Obrador administration’s current proposal on security and militarization and what are its possible implications?\nLet’s start with the context. Mexico faces high levels of insecurity and violence, with more than 35,000 homicides registered annually, a huge increase in the last 15 years, and more than 100,000 people recognized as missing or disappeared. Violence, including homicides, disappearances, and other forms of violence, has become a sadly common tool used by different actors and groups to impose their will, send messages, and seek to control illegal markets and territories.\nIn this context, President López Obrador says that the deployment of military forces in police tasks throughout the country must be expanded and made practically permanent. He says that the armed forces are the only ones who can take care of this serious problem, and that local and civilian institutions can’t handle it.\nThis is the same argument that Mexico’s federal government has pushed for the last decade and a half, at least, with disastrous security and human rights consequences. When Felipe Calderón became president in 2006, he announced militarization as a temporary measure that would supposedly allow the government to strengthen police and other institutions and regain territorial control in certain areas of the country. What actually happened was that military deployment was prolonged throughout the Calderón administration, and then continued with the administration of President Enrique Peña Nieto.\n2. But before López Obrador was elected in 2018 he had promised to return the armed forces to their barracks…\nYes, that’s what he had promised prior to being elected, but, on the contrary, one of his first actions was the creation of a National Guard to replace the Federal Police. This National Guard was constitutionally established as a civilian security force, but it has been managed from the start as a military force. The vast majority of its more than 100,000 members are military and, in practice, it is already under the operational control of Sedena. And that’s not all: in addition to the National Guard, the armed forces (the army and the navy) are still deployed. So today, military deployment is at its highest in recent history.\nWith López Obrador, we can see certain changes in the way the armed forces are used. Armed confrontations between security forces and civilians, for example, have dropped in absolute terms. The strategy, especially with the National Guard, has been to increase the territorial presence of state forces, which may be relevant, especially in certain areas, but it is far from enough to have a significant impact on the situation of insecurity and crime.\nThere’s another change with López Obrador: he doesn’t speak of militarization as a temporary measure, but rather has insisted that the National Guard become part of Sedena, that it become an armed force, so that there is no longer any federal civilian police force in Mexico. He says that what he wants is to make it very difficult or impossible for future governments to reverse this model. And he has also said in recent weeks that the deployment of the army and the navy must also continue long term, beyond 2024, which is currently the deadline established by a transitory article of the constitutional reform that created the National Guard in 2019.\n3. This could have strong implications for the future in Mexico.\nOf course. López Obrador has already sent a bill to the Mexican Congress to reform a series of laws to give Sedena operational and administrative control of the National Guard, seeking to make it a body made up entirely of military personnel, which could even assist the armed forces in purely military missions.\nThis goes against the Constitution of Mexico, which, in article 21, establishes that the National Guard is a civilian police institution.\n4. What implications do these proposals have for the power and role of the armed forces\nvis-à-vis\nthe civilian government?\nWhat these proposals seem to seek is to deepen the government’s clear tendency to grant more and more civilian functions and budget, and thus more power, to military institutions, especially Sedena.\nUnder this model, the armed forces are not only deployed in policing tasks, but also have a very important role in migration control tasks at the borders, are in charge of ports and customs, and build and manage large infrastructure projects, among others.\nIt would seem that, instead of investing in the consolidation of the institutions that any democratic government should have, the answer is to skip that necessary step and deploy the armed forces instead. Certainly, militarization seems to offer an easy or quick way to present solutions to the population and say, “look, I’m taking firm actions, this is how we’re going to end corruption, this is how we’re going to provide security”, but all the evidence so far tells us that the desired results will not be achieved this way. On the contrary, this path of militarization is intensifying a dynamic in which the Mexican armed forces, which have always enjoyed a worrying degree of autonomy and lack of transparency and accountability, will now have more and more power\nvis-à-vis\ncivilian authorities.\n5. What consequences could this proposal have in terms of human rights, specifically for migrants, women, and indigenous people, who have been some of the most affected historically by militarization?\nPresident López Obrador has said that he has given instructions for the armed forces not to repress and not to violate human rights. Of course, even with instructions, there continue to be cases of excessive use of force and arbitrary deprivation of life by members of the armed forces and the National Guard during his government.\nAt the same time, available data show a reduction, in absolute terms, in the levels of serious human rights violations such as torture, enforced disappearances, and extrajudicial executions as compared to ten years ago, for example, during the Calderón administration.\nWhat we have to remember, however, is that these military institutions are the same ones responsible for the levels of abuses seen just a few years ago and they have not undergone any effective accountability process. Nor have they been reformed to guarantee transparency and build effective control mechanisms. In the case of the National Guard, for example, the government has even refused to comply with a ruling of the Inter-American Court of Human Rights that mandated the creation of an external oversight mechanism.\nAdditionally, neither the National Human Rights Commission nor the National Prosecutor’s Office have been effective control mechanisms in terms of investigations of military abuses.\n6. What public policies should Mexico implement to effectively address the current situation of violence and insecurity?\n\nViolence and the operational capacity of criminal groups persist in Mexico essentially because the country has not consolidated a reliable, capable, and well-resourced criminal investigation system that solves and punishes crimes and can investigate complex criminal phenomena.\nSecurity and justice institutions operate, especially in the states, in often precarious conditions, with deep-rooted problems of lack of capacity, but also frequently of corruption. This contributes to the fact that the overwhelming majority of crimes committed against the population go unpunished.\nThe key pending task, and I’m not saying anything new here, is to strengthen and reform civil institutions so that the country has a system capable of responding to and preventing crimes. I’m not saying there are no efforts and projects in that direction, but so far militarization has captured political and financial resources while the strengthening of civilian institutions does not receive sufficient attention.\n7. What role could the United States play in the implementation of these measures and what opportunities exist for these types of policies in the current context of the Bicentennial Framework that governs bilateral cooperation?\nThere are many commitments and opportunities. The question is how to ensure that bilateral cooperation not only generates positive actions, but also overcomes obstacles on both sides of the border that interfere with the progress that could be made.\nIn 2021, the United States and Mexico replaced the “Merida Initiative” with the “Bicentennial Framework,” which promises a public health approach to drugs and actions to reduce the violence affecting the population.\nIf you read the announcement of the Bicentennial Framework, you might almost think that militarization no longer exists because almost the entire text speaks of other types of actions. But what exists is rather a gap between the areas and actions of cooperation foreseen in the agreement, and the Mexican federal government’s main security strategy. That is going to be an increasingly acute problem if the National Guard is formally placed under the control of Sedena and there are no longer any remnants of civilian police institutions at the federal level, because the U.S. Congress has also instructed that rule of law cooperation funds for Mexico should not be used to support the participation of military forces in police tasks.\nNow, on the U.S. side, the Bicentennial Framework was adopted in the midst of a broader context in which the Biden administration’s priority for Mexico is for the Mexican government to take steps to reduce the number of migrants and asylum seekers arriving at the U.S. southern border. The weight and priority given to the issue of immigration control can only make it more difficult to address other issues with the Mexican government, especially uncomfortable issues.\nSo, both governments need to move towards comprehensive cooperation on migration issues that prioritizes human rights and international protection, more along the lines of the Declaration adopted by the countries of the region at the Summit of the Americas last June, for example. But in addition, both sides need to collaborate to overcome errors and implement effective models in other areas, such as public security strategies.\nShare\nShare\nNewsletter\nSubscribe to get the latest on analysis and policy\nSubscribe\nDo you find our content useful and informative? Do you frequently visit the WOLA website? If so, please consider making a donation today to keep our website free and open to all.\nDonate\nRelated Analysis\nView all\nLatin America\n,\nUnited States\nDemocracy\n,\nDrug Policy\n,", "excerpt_sha256": "5ba324bffd3ad1161d177a47042f46434eab008a03799eca8868158b651dca7a", "verification_status": "corrected", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt supports insufficient attention to civilian-institution strengthening, not specifically insufficient investment."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0049-E005", "source_id": "SRC-03fd4f3bb212441b", "url": "https://spcommreports.ohchr.org/TMResultsBase/DownLoadPublicCommunicationFile?gId=28547", "title": "OHCHR communication AL MEX (9.2023)", "publisher": "UN Special Procedures", "raw_sha256": "c7cfae2229edba41e48c7c3e82917814e7598ea7c1ac7f4e192f1fe908687a78", "fact_summary": "UN special procedures communicated allegations of repeated killings of civilians by military or National Guard personnel in Nuevo Laredo and alleged delayed, non-transparent, partial, or military-led investigations, scene manipulation, intimidation of victims’ associates, and impunity. The communication notes that on 7 June 2023 AMLO stated an investigation had begun and that responsible personnel were about to be transferred to competent authorities; SEDENA later announced 16 military arrests and proceedings, while the communication stated that there was no record of competent civilian authorities participating in the investigation.", "question_ids": ["3B.1", "3B.10", "3B.2", "3B.4", "3B.9", "4B.4", "8B.9"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The communication was issued on 24 October 2023 and covers incidents from 2021-2023, including 2023 presidential and military responses.", "ruler_attribution": "The president publicly stated that an investigation had begun; the alleged operational failures concern state security and investigative institutions.", "limitations": ["The UN communication expressly states that it does not prejudge the truth of the allegations.", "It does not establish whether AMLO personally directed, knew of, or corrected the alleged abuses beyond the cited public statement."], "start_unit": 1, "end_unit": 7, "locator": "page 1 through page 7", "exact_excerpt": "PALAIS DES NATIONS • 1211 GENEVA 10, SWITZERLAND\n\n\n\n\n  Mandatos del Relator Especial sobre ejecuciones extrajudiciales, sumarias o arbitrarias y del\n                       Grupo de Trabajo sobre la Detención Arbitraria\n\nRef.: AL MEX 9/2023\n(por favor, utilice esta referencia en su respuesta)\n                                                                                24 de octubre de 2023\n\nExcelencia,\n\n       Tenemos el honor de dirigirnos a Usted en nuestra calidad de Relator Especial\nsobre ejecuciones extrajudiciales, sumarias o arbitrarias y de Grupo de Trabajo sobre\nla Detención Arbitraria, de conformidad con las resoluciones 53/4 y 51/8 del Consejo\nde Derechos Humanos.\n\n        En este contexto, quisiéramos señalar a la atención urgente del Gobierno de Su\nExcelencia la información que hemos recibido en relación con asesinatos de\npersonas civiles en el municipio de Nuevo Laredo, Tamaulipas, por parte de\nagentes de las fuerzas militares entre 2021 y 2023 y la presunta falta de\ninvestigaciones prontas, transparentes, imparciales y efectivas sobre estos\nhechos. Estos actos habrían ocurrido en un contexto de alto nivel de impunidad en el\nque las denuncias de abusos a los derechos humanos por parte de las fuerzas militares\nhabrían aumentado continuamente.\n\n        En comunicaciones enviadas con anterioridad al Gobierno de su Excelencia,\nun grupo de procedimientos especiales ya había expresado preocupación por los\nasesinatos de personas civiles por agentes de las fuerzas militares o policía y la\nmilitarización de la política de seguridad (MEX 5/2022, enviada en la fecha del 17 de\noctubre de 2022; MEX 11/2022 enviada en la fecha de 23 de septiembre de 2022 y\nMEX 9/2021 enviada en la fecha de 5 de julio de 2021). Agradecemos las respuestas\nrecibidas con fecha 14 de diciembre de 2022 y 3 de septiembre de 2021, relativas a las\ncomunicaciones MEX 5/2022 y MEX 9/2021. Lamentamos, sin embargo, no haber\nrecibido aún respuesta a las demás comunicaciones.\n\n        Según la información recibida:\n\n        Antecedentes\n\n        El alto número de homicidios cometidos en México, incluyendo los cometidos\n        por autoridades, sería el resultado de la implementación de una política de\n        combate a las organizaciones criminales basada en la militarización de las\n        fuerzas de seguridad que vendría afectando de manera muy grave a la\n        población civil.\n\n        Alegaciones de ejecuciones arbitrarias de cinco civiles en Nuevo Laredo,\n        Tamaulipas\n\n        El 18 de mayo de 2023, fuerzas militares habrían asesinado a cinco personas\n        civiles en lo que posteriormente se denominó un contexto de fuego cruzado.\n\n        Una cámara de seguridad de un centro comercial en Nuevo Laredo,\n        Tamaulipas, habría mostrado a los militares sacando a civiles de una\n        camioneta. Algunos de los civiles portarían armas. Los soldados los habrían\n\ndesarmado, pateado y luego colocado junto a una pared. Minutos después, los\nsoldados habrían disparado contra los civiles. Salvo uno, que más tarde habría\nmuerto en el hospital, los civiles habrían muerto en el lugar de los hechos.\n\nLa cámara de seguridad habría mostrado además cómo los soldados, con los\ncinco individuos ya muertos, habrían alterado la escena del crimen. Uno de los\nagentes habría cogido las armas largas con una bolsa roja para evitar tocarlas\ncon las manos y dejar huellas, y las habría colocado junto a los cadáveres.\nOtro agente habría quitado las esposas que llevaría una de las personas\nasesinadas. Paramédicos habrían llegado al lugar de los hechos para llevarse a\nuno de los individuos que posteriormente habría fallecido en el hospital. La\nFiscalía no llegaría al lugar de los hechos hasta horas más tarde.\n\nTras el incidente, el oficial a cargo del convoy militar, un teniente de\nInfantería habría indicado a la Fiscalía encargada de las investigaciones, que él\ny sus compañeros estaban desarmando a los civiles, cuando éstos los atacaron.\nSegún el oficial, los soldados habrían respondido con disparos.\n\nEl 7 de junio de 2023, el Presidente de México afirmó que ya se había iniciado\nel proceso de investigación y que los militares responsables estaban a punto de\nser entregados a las autoridades competentes.\n\nEl 9 de junio de 2023, dieciséis personas detenidas fueron vinculadas a\nproceso y se dictó prisión preventiva como medida cautelar contra ellas, por lo\nque permanecerían en el Reclusorio Militar de la Primera Región Militar,\nubicado dentro del Campo Militar Número 1-A, en la Ciudad de México.\n\nEl 10 de junio de 2023, la Secretaría de la Defensa Nacional (SEDENA)\nanunció en un comunicado que los dieciséis militares involucrados en la\npresunta ejecución extrajudicial de las cinco personas civiles habrían sido\nenviados a juicio. La SEDENA explicó que el 7 de junio de 2023 se inició una\ninvestigación de oficio, y un día después la Fiscalía General de Justicia Militar\nhabría librado dieciséis órdenes de aprehensión contra los militares\ninvolucrados, por presuntamente configurarse delitos contrarios a la disciplina\nmilitar.\n\nNo se tiene constancia de que autoridades civiles, que serían las competentes\npara investigar estos casos, hayan participado en la investigación.\n\nPresunta ejecución de cinco personas civiles en la colonia Manuel Cavazos\nLerma, Nuevo Laredo\n\nEl 26 de febrero de 2023, en horas de la madrugada, un grupo de siete jóvenes\nque había salido de un centro nocturno en la colonia Manuel Cavazos Lerma,\nNuevo Laredo, habría sido interceptado por soldados del Ejército Mexicano,\naparentemente por no detenerse ante un llamado de las autoridades. Como\nconsecuencia de la intercepción, cinco individuos habrían fallecido en el lugar\ny dos más habrían sobrevivido con graves lesiones físicas y mentales. Al\nmenos dos jóvenes habrían recibido disparos en la nuca mientras yacían en la\nacera.\n\n\n\n\n                                    2\n\nSegún el informe inicial elaborado por un capitán de las fuerzas armadas, al\nmenos cuatro militares habrían disparado sus armas de fuego sin \"orden\nsuperior\", por lo que habrían sido detenidos horas después de ser imputados\nante la Fiscalía de Justicia Militar por el delito de desobediencia.\n\nEn el lugar de los hechos, las autoridades habrían contabilizado más de un\ncentenar de impactos de bala disparados por los efectivos del Ejército\nMexicano tanto en la camioneta Chevrolet que trasladaba a las personas\nheridas como en los cuerpos de las personas fallecidas y en un par de casas\nubicadas frente al lugar donde murieron los cinco jóvenes.\n\nCasi 72 horas después del incidente, ninguna autoridad federal, estatal o\nmunicipal habría informado oficialmente de los hechos acontecidos.\n\nEl 10 de abril de 2023, un Juez del Centro de Justicia Penal Federal con sede\nen Reynosa, Tamaulipas, imputó a los mismos cuatro militares por los delitos\nde homicidio calificado y tentativa de homicidio calificado, en la causa\n46/2023. Estos cuatro Cabos de Caballería, pertenecientes al Regimiento XVI\nde Caballería con sede en Nuevo Laredo, estarían enfrentando su proceso\npenal recluidos en el Reclusorio Militar Número 1 de la Ciudad de México.\n\nDe acuerdo con la información recibida, las entidades gubernamentales no\nhabrían investigado la cadena de mando. Personas relacionadas con las\npersonas presuntamente asesinadas que intentaron presionar para que se\ninvestigaran estos incidentes habrían sido objeto de actos de intimidación y\namenazas.\n\nCasos adicionales de presuntas ejecuciones extrajudiciales en el municipio de\nNuevo Laredo\n\nEl 16 de abril de 2023, una familia que viajaba en una camioneta Chevrolet\nSuburban fue presuntamente tiroteada por personal militar de la Guardia\nNacional en el kilómetro 9 del Libramiento Vial Mex II. El tiroteo habría\ncausado la muerte de una mujer embarazada, su hijo nonato de ocho meses y\notro civil adulto.\n\nEl 21 de noviembre de 2022, una mujer de entre 25 y 30 años y una mujer de\nentre 15 y 20 años habrían sido privadas de libertad por civiles armados que\nviajaban en una camioneta Chevrolet Tahoe negra y que habrían intentado\nescapar de una intercepción militar.\n\nEn el enfrentamiento con los agentes militares, estas mujeres habrían sido\nbaleadas por los militares en el cruce de Héroe de Nacataz y Tomàs F. de la\nGarza, Colonia Palacios.\n\nAl llegar personal de la Fiscalía General de la República (FGR), habrían\nencontrado que estas mujeres estaban muertas en el asiento trasero de la\ncamioneta Tahoe y presentaban huellas de haber sido atadas por las muñecas.\nAl menos tres civiles varones más murieron en el operativo del Ejército\nMexicano.\n\n\n\n\n                                  3\n\nEl 31 de agosto de 2022, una menor de cuatro años se dirigía al Hospital del\nSeguro Social acompañada de sus familiares, quienes se habrían dado cuenta\nde un bloqueo en el cruce de las calles 20 de noviembre y Santos Degollado,\ndonde varios vehículos militares bloqueaban el paso a la circulación debido a\nuna supuesta agresión en su contra. Al momento de cambiar la ruta hacia el\nhospital, el vehículo que conducían habría sido objeto de al menos tres\ndisparos que ingresaron por la parte trasera del vehículo, uno de ellos habría\nimpactado en la cabeza de la menor causándole la muerte instantánea.\n\nA la FGR se le habrían proporcionado al menos tres videos que indicarían la\npresencia de militares al paso del automóvil, pero el Ministerio Público se\nhabría negado a concluir la carpeta de investigación argumentando falta de\npruebas contra los integrantes del Ejército Mexicano responsables de los\ndisparos.\n\nEl 28 de junio de 2022, aproximadamente a las 15:00 horas, se habrían\nescuchado varios disparos en el fraccionamiento Lomas del Río cuando una\ncamioneta pick up verde habría sido rodeada por efectivos de la Guardia\nNacional, quienes se habrían encontrado apostados en la calle Medellín, entre\nDionicio Carreón y Jesús Guajardo. Los ocupantes de la camioneta verde\nhabrían detenido su vehículo frente a una casa cercana y sacado las manos por\nuna de las ventanas gritando a la Guardia Nacional \"no disparen más\", al\nparecer en señal de rendición. Posteriormente, agentes del Ejército mexicano\nhabrían llegado al lugar y habrían comenzado a disparar contra los ocupantes\nde la camioneta verde, al parecer sin que los civiles les atacaran, causándoles\nla muerte. Residentes de la zona habrían gritado a los soldados y al personal de\nla Guardia Nacional que dejaran de disparar contra los ocupantes de la\ncamioneta verde. Tras los incidentes, los agentes de la Guardia Nacional se\nhabrían retirado del lugar. Los miembros del personal del Ejército Mexicano\nhabrían movido o arrastrado la camioneta verde debajo de un árbol.\n\nEl 7 de febrero de 2021, aproximadamente a las 23:40 horas, cuatro civiles\ncirculaban por la calle Cerro de La Silla, esquina con la calle Sierra de Las\nÁguilas. En ese momento, una camioneta de la Secretaría de la Defensa\nNacional (SEDENA) con varios agentes, quienes, sin mediar comandos de voz\no luz, habrían disparado sus armas de fuego en contra de los civiles, privando\nde la vida a uno de ellos y lesionando con proyectiles de arma de fuego a otros\ndos, quienes junto con el cuarto civil habrían sido puestos a disposición de la\nFGR.\n\nEl vehículo circulaba por la colonia Colinas del Sur cuando habría sido\nperseguido por varios vehículos militares que le cerraron el paso y le\ndispararon. Los militares habrían afirmado haber sido atacados desde el\nautomóvil conducido por los civiles. No habría rastros de disparos desde el\nauto de los civiles, y la prueba de rodizonato de sodio habría sido negativa. Al\nmenos dieciséis militares de la Base Operativa Conjunta de La Ribereña\nhabrían participado en el incidente, viajando en tres vehículos Cheyenne y un\nSandCat. Sin embargo, sólo dos habrían sido identificados.\n\nEn este caso, se habría abierto un proceso judicial contra sólo dos militares,\nimputándoles cargos, pero desertaron y no habrían sido encontrados para\ncumplir su condena.\n\n\n                                   4\n\nEl 10 de marzo de 2021, por la tarde, dos civiles viajaban en una camioneta\n       Yukón modelo 2003 hacia la Cruz Roja. Alrededor de las 17:00 horas, en el\n       cruce de Santos Degollado y Gutiérrez, habrían sido atacados a balazos por\n       soldados del Ejército Mexicano, quienes viajaban en dos camionetas y un\n       camión blindado. Como resultado del incidente, uno de los civiles habría\n       recibido un disparo en la nuca y otro en el hombro, lo que los habría obligado\n       a detener el vehículo. Los soldados habrían procedido a registrar el camión de\n       las víctimas en lugar de prestarles asistencia médica. Los paramédicos del\n       Cuerpo de Bomberos habrían trasladado al herido al Hospital General, pero\n       éste fallecería pocos minutos después debido a la gravedad de las lesiones.\n\n       Irregularidades en la investigación e impunidad\n\n       De acuerdo con las informaciones suministradas, todos los casos presentados\n       en esta comunicación indicarían prácticas documentadas de manipulación de\n       pruebas, alteración de la escena del crimen y el impedimento a las autoridades\n       civiles, así como a los familiares, de acceder al lugar de los hechos hasta que\n       la orden del responsable militar lo autorizara. En estas circunstancias, de\n       nuevo según las alegaciones, los agentes de las fuerzas estatales dispondrían\n       de entre tres y seis horas para manipular la escena de un crimen y un alto\n       número de investigaciones se verían retrasadas y resultarían parciales.\n\n       Por otra parte, en relación con las investigaciones de homicidios dolosos\n       cometidos por servidores públicos, la FGR habría proporcionado información\n       inconsistente de manera recurrente. La FGR habría indicado que entre enero\n       de 2006 y noviembre de 2021 sólo se abrieron 50 averiguaciones previas o\n       carpetas de investigación por este delito, de las cuales sólo 16 fueron\n       judicializadas y sólo una resultó en sentencia. Adicionalmente, en al menos\n       uno de los casos mencionados en esta comunicación, las autoridades militares\n       serían quienes habrían iniciado las investigaciones criminales, cuando las\n       autoridades competentes para investigar y procesar los hechos serían las\n       autoridades civiles.\n\n       Organizaciones de la sociedad civil reportaron 287,254 carpetas de\n       investigación abiertas en México por el delito de homicidio doloso y\n       feminicidio entre 2011 y 2022. Sin embargo, sólo habría 188 carpetas\n       judicializadas contra servidores públicos por este delito.\n\n        Sin pretender prejuzgar la veracidad de estas alegaciones o formular una\nconclusión sobre los hechos, quisiéramos expresar nuestra profunda preocupación\nante los asesinatos de personas civiles en el municipio de Nuevo Laredo, Tamaulipas,\npor parte de agentes de las fuerzas de seguridad del Estado entre 2021 y 2023 y la\naparente falta de investigaciones prontas, transparentes, imparciales y efectivas sobre\nestos hechos por parte de autoridades civiles.\n\n       De confirmarse los hechos alegados anteriormente, supondrían una violación\nde múltiples disposiciones consagradas en la Declaración Universal de Derechos\nHumanos (DUDH), el Pacto Internacional de Derechos Civiles y Políticos (PIDCP), la\nConvención sobre los Derechos del Niño (CDN) y la Convención sobre la eliminación\nde todas las formas de discriminación contra la mujer (CEDAW), ratificadas por\nMéxico el 23 de marzo de 1981, el 21 de septiembre de 1990 y el 23 de marzo de\n\n\n                                          5\n\n1981, respectivamente.\n\n           Consternados por el hecho de que estos asesinatos hayan implicado también a\n    menores, subrayamos asimismo la importancia de instaurar medidas especiales de\n    protección de las niñas y los niños contra cualquier violación de su derecho a la vida,\n    ya que se encuentran en circunstancias de especial vulnerabilidad.\n\n            Consideramos alarmantes los informes sobre el alto nivel de impunidad en\n    relación con las ejecuciones extrajudiciales denunciadas en México y subrayamos la\n    necesidad de investigaciones y enjuiciamientos transparentes, independientes y\n    rápidos, a cargo de las autoridades competentes, que garanticen el derecho de las\n    víctimas a la verdad, la justicia, la reparación y la no repetición. Resaltamos que es\n    fundamental llevar a cabo las investigaciones sobre las muertes documentadas de\n    conformidad con las normas internacionales pertinentes, incluido la versión revisada\n    del Manual de las Naciones Unidas sobre la Prevención e Investigación Eficaces de\n    las Ejecuciones Extralegales, Arbitrarias o Sumarias (El Protocolo de Minnesota\n    sobre la Investigación de Muertes Potencialmente Ilícitas (2016)),1 y deben tener por\n    objeto, inter alía, extraer las lecciones necesarias para revisar las prácticas y las\n    políticas con miras a evitar que se repitan las violaciones. Asimismo, recordamos que\n    es de suma importancia incorporar una perspectiva de género en todas las etapas de la\n    investigación criminal, y que las investigaciones conduzcan a la sanción de los autores\n    identificados de los asesinatos denunciados, incluyendo, si es pertinente, a sus\n    superiores responsables de dar las órdenes en las circunstancias denunciadas.\n\n            En relación con la denuncia del uso excesivo de la fuerza letal por parte de\n    agentes del Estado, destacamos que las medidas para proteger la vida deben incluir\n    una “legislación que controle el uso de fuerza letal por parte de los funcionarios\n    encargados de hacer cumplir la ley, procedimientos para garantizar que las\n    operaciones de aplicación de la ley minimizan los riesgos para la vida, notificación e\n    investigación obligatorias de incidentes letales y que pongan en peligro la vida, y\n    proporcionar a los funcionarios responsables del control de multitudes equipos de\n    protección eficaces y medios de fuerza menos letales”.2\n\n            Expresamos nuestra profunda preocupación por la gravedad de la situación de\n    ejecuciones arbitrarias por parte de las Fuerzas Armadas Mexicanas, especialmente en\n    el municipio de Nuevo Laredo, Tamaulipas, y por los patrones observados en las\n    ejecuciones registradas. En este contexto, recomendamos al Gobierno de su\n    Excelencia que se establezcan mecanismos externos, independientes y civiles que\n    garanticen la rendición de cuentas de las Fuerzas Armadas, particularmente en casos\n    de violaciones graves de derechos humanos cometidas por militares en contra de\n    civiles y cuando realicen actividades que no correspondan a la disciplina militar.\n\n          En relación con las alegaciones arriba mencionadas, sírvase encontrar adjunto\n    el Anexo de referencias al derecho internacional de los derechos humanos el cual\n    resume los instrumentos y principios internacionales pertinentes.\n\n\n\n\n    ––––––––––––––––––––––––––\n1   https://www.ohchr.org/sites/default/files/Documents/Publications/MinnesotaProtocol_SP.pdf\n2   Comité de Derechos Humanos, Comentario General General n°36, parr. 13.\n\n\n                                                          6\n\nEs nuestra responsabilidad, de acuerdo con los mandatos que nos han sido\notorgados por el Consejo de Derechos Humanos, intentar clarificar las alegaciones\nllevadas a nuestra atención. En este sentido, estaríamos muy agradecidos/as de tener\nsu cooperación y sus observaciones sobre los asuntos siguientes:\n\n       1.     Sírvase proporcionar cualquier información o comentario adicional en\n              relación con las alegaciones mencionadas arriba.\n\n       2.     Sírvase proporcionar información detallada sobre cualquier\n              investigación realizada en torno a los asesinatos de personas civiles en\n              el municipio de Nuevo Laredo, Tamaulipas, por agentes de las fuerzas\n              militares. Por favor incluya información sobre el estado actual de estas\n              investigaciones y las autoridades a cargo de las mismas, sobre el grado\n              en que personas asociadas a las víctimas han tenido acceso a la\n              información sobre los avances de la investigación, y si han podido o\n              podrán participar en las investigaciones.\n\n       3.     Sírvase indicar si se ha llevado a cabo algún examen forense de los\n              cuerpos de las personas asesinadas, y si dicho examen se ha realizado\n              de conformidad con las normas internacionales y científicos aplicables\n              en casos de muertes potencialmente ilícitas, especialmente con el\n              Protocolo de Minnesota de las Naciones Unidas sobre la investigación\n              de muertes potencialmente ilícitas.\n\n       4.     Sírvase proporcionar información detallada sobre las medidas que\n              protejan el derecho a la vida y promuevan la justicia, la rendición de\n              cuentas y el derecho a la reparación mediante la promoción de una\n              investigación eficaz de toda muerte potencialmente ilícita, de acuerdo\n              con los Principios Relativos a una Eficaz Prevención e Investigación de\n              las Ejecuciones Extralegales, Arbitrarias o Sumarias y al Protocolo de\n              Minnesota.\n\n       5.     Sírvase informar si se ha iniciado un proceso de reparación integral y\n              de rehabilitación a las víctimas sobrevivientes y a las y los familiares\n              de las víctimas fallecidas.\n\n       6.     Sírvase informar sobre qué medidas de protección les han sido\n              otorgadas a las personas que habrían sido objeto de actos de\n              intimidación y hostigamiento por su trabajo de reivindicar\n              investigaciones y justicia con respecto a los casos de asesinatos en el\n              municipio de Nuevo Laredo, Tamaulipas, si tales medidas fueron\n              implementadas de forma efectiva y si se iniciaron investigaciones para\n              evaluar la responsabilidad de quienes estaban a cargo de la seguridad\n              de estas personas.\n\n       7.     Sírvase informar sobre las medidas de protección tomadas para\n              asegurar la integridad física y psicológica de las niñas y los niños, así\n              como de mujeres, que se encuentren en situaciones de particular\n              vulnerabilidad, en el contexto del número elevado de ejecuciones\n              extrajudiciales en México.\n\n\n\n\n                                          7", "excerpt_sha256": "cf44d8c11b0e018ab59aadc48695aa3fb8279bd2507dc25fdc749dd7edf553df", "verification_status": "corrected", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt uses the formulation that there was no record of civilian-authority participation; it does not use the broader phrase 'found no evidence.'"]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0049-E011", "source_id": "SRC-4b42f64b3377abb8", "url": "https://hchr.org.mx/wp/wp-content/uploads/2023/09/Mexico-Preliminary-Findings-29-September-FINAL-English-.pdf", "title": "Working Group on Arbitrary Detention: Preliminary Findings from its Visit to Mexico", "publisher": "United Nations Working Group on Arbitrary Detention", "raw_sha256": "5a232141559d7ced98b62e64855e7ba536321cc5f26440b0f8f96d868e0fb26b", "fact_summary": "During a September 2023 visit invited by Mexico, the UN Working Group on Arbitrary Detention reported full and unimpeded access and acknowledged reforms including the accusatory criminal justice model, a National Registry of Detentions, restrictions on military jurisdiction, and releases. It nevertheless stated that arbitrary detention remained widespread, citing mandatory pre-trial detention, militarized policing linked to violence and torture, unresolved complaints, excessive arrest violence, deficient records, pressure on judges, weak public defense, poor detention conditions, prolonged migrant and child detention contrary to stated legal limits, discrimination against indigenous people, and negligible treatment for people with psychosocial disabilities.", "question_ids": ["3B.1", "3B.10", "3B.2", "3B.4", "3B.5", "3B.6", "3B.7", "3B.9", "4B.4", "6B.3", "6B.5", "6B.8", "6B.9", "8B.10", "8B.6"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "Directly covers the Working Group's September 2023 country visit and conditions observed or reported during that period.", "ruler_attribution": "The findings concern federal and state authorities under AMLO's administration; the invitation and official cooperation are specifically attributed to the government.", "limitations": ["The findings are preliminary and include information received from officials, civil society, and detainees.", "They describe systemic conditions across multiple levels of government and do not assign personal responsibility to AMLO for each failure."], "start_unit": 1, "end_unit": 10, "locator": "page 1 through page 10", "exact_excerpt": "Working Group on Arbitrary Detention: Preliminary Findings from its visit to Mexico (18 to 29 Sep-\ntember 2023)\nIntroduction\nAt the invitation of the Government of Mexico, the United Nations Working Group on Arbitrary Detention\nconducted an official visit to Mexico from 18 to 29 September 2023. The Working Group was represented by\nMr. Matthew Gillett (New Zealand), Ms. Ganna Yudkivska (Ukraine), and Ms. Miriam Estrada-Castillo\n(Ecuador), and accompanied by staff of the Office of the UN High Commissioner for Human Rights.\nThe Working Group first visited Mexico in 2002, approximately 20 years before this second official visit of\n2023. The Working Group would like to express its gratitude to the Government of Mexico for its invitation\nto undertake the country visit and for its cooperation. During the visit, the Working Group met with the\nofficials of the following Federal authorities: Ministry of the Interior, Ministry of Foreign Affairs, Ministry of\nHealth, Ministry of Security and Citizens’ Protection, Ministry of Defense, Ministry of the Navy, Attorney-\nGeneral’s Office, Supreme Court, Council of the Federal Judiciary, Federal Institute of Public Defender, the\nCongress of the Union, the Decentralized administrative body for the prevention and social rehabilitation, the\nNational Migration Institute, the National System for the Protection of Children and Adolescents, the National\nCommission to Prevent and Eradicate Violence against Women, the National Institute for Indigenous People,\nthe Commission for Dialogue with Indigenous Peoples and National Human Rights Commission, as well as\nauthorities in the states of Mexico City, Nuevo León and Chiapas.\nThe Working Group would like to thank the OHCHR Mexico Office and their staff as well as the UN Country\nTeam and the Resident Coordinator for supporting the visit. The Working Group also recognizes the numerous\nstakeholders from civil society and interviewees who shared their perspectives on the arbitrary deprivation of\nliberty. The Group thanks all of them for the information and assistance they provided.\nThese observations constitute the Working Group’s preliminary findings from its country visit. They will\nserve as a basis for future deliberations between the members of the Working Group at its forthcoming sessions\nin Geneva. The Working Group will submit its report to the UN Human Rights Council in September 2024.\nDuring the country visit, the Working Group enjoyed full and unimpeded access and went to 4 federal facilities\nand 11 State and municipal facilities. These included penitentiaries (both men’s and women’s at the federal\nand state level), police stations, migration detention centres, adolescent centres, child protection shelters,\nmental health hospitals and prosecutors’ offices. It confidentially interviewed around 173 persons deprived of\ntheir liberty. The Working Group received unimpeded access to all places it sought to visit and it expresses it\nappreciation to the Federal and States Governments for their cooperation throughout the country visit.\n1. Good practices and positive developments\nThe Working Group welcomes the constitutional, legal and regulatory reforms, introduced in Mexico since\nits 2002 visit. After the Working Group’s first visit, Mexico has ratified several international human rights\ntreaties, including on the International Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities, the Inter-\nAmerican Convention on the Protection of human Rights of Older Persons, and the Optional Protocol to the\nUN Convention against Torture and Other Cruel, Inhuman or Degrading Treatment or Punishment (OPCAT)\non 11 April 2005. In 2007, the National Preventive Mechanism against Torture was created a as part of the\nNational Human Rights Commission. The system of regular, independent monitoring of all places of\ndeprivation of liberty that OPCAT puts in place is not only a crucial tool for the prevention of torture and ill-\ntreatment, but also for combating the arbitrary deprivation of liberty. Equally, the Working Group commends\nMexico’s 2008 ratification of the International Convention for the Protection of All Persons from Enforced\nDisappearance and, since 2020, the acceptance of the individual communications procedure. The Working\nGroup notes that enforced disappearances constitute an aggravated form of arbitrary detention. Additionally,\nthe Working Group notes the following positive developments to prevent the occurrence of arbitrary detention.\n1\n\n1.1 Amendments to consolidate a human rights-centred and accusatory model of criminal justice\nIn 2008, Mexico transitioned to an accusatory model of criminal justice, which led to a significant reduction\nof practices that violated human rights. The presumption of innocence, now prescribed as a foundational\nprinciple, is instrumental for the avoidance of arbitrary detention. The new framework also provides that\ndefendants have the right to appear before a judge and has reinforced victims’ rights. Constitutional\nAmendments in 2011 placed human rights front and centre in the Constitution. The Working Group also notes\nimprovements introduced pursuant to the 2016 Law on the Execution of Penalties and commends the increased\nuse of conciliation boards to facilitate early release, for example in Chiapas. However, the Working Group\nemphasizes that Mexico must bring its Constitution and laws into conformity with all of its international\nhuman rights obligations, as detailed in these findings.1\n1.2 Introduction of a National Registry of Detentions\nIn 2019, Mexico introduced a constitutional reform which established that the authorities must maintain a\nNational Registry of Detentions. This accords with the Working Group’s previous recommendation that a\nproper detention registration system is essential for preventing arbitrary detention.2 The Ministry of Security\nand Citizen’s Protection oversees the National Registry of Detentions. Almost 2,000,000 searches of the\ndatabase have been made by members of the public, and around 30 percent of those searches revealed that the\nperson was in detention. Detentions must be entered into the registry within 5 hours of deprivation of liberty.\nNonetheless, the National Registry does not capture all detentions. In particular, administrative detentions of\nmigrants and persons with psycho-social disabilities are not included. This creates an information gap which\nreduces the authorities’ ability to prevent and redress arbitrary detention of migrants and other vulnerable\npersons. Moreover, whereas the Supreme Court held that military personnel, including those incorporated into\nthe National Guard, must enter detentions into the Registry, several exceptions to this requirement persist. The\nWorking Group emphasises that authorities must ensure that all officials carrying detentions of any nature,\nincluding those in the Army, Navy and National Guard, are required to enter those detentions into the National\nRegister and that all officials, civilian and military, are aware of this responsibility.\n1.3 Restriction of administration of justice by military courts\nIn its 2002 country visit report on Mexico, the Working Group cautioned that military courts should not be\nused to adjudicate military members accused of crimes ordinarily dealt with by civilian courts, such as drug-\ntrafficking and enforced disappearance. In 2014, Mexico amended its laws to require that military members\nwho commit crimes against civilians are tried before civilian courts. The Working Group considers this is a\npositive means of reducing the risk of impunity among military members for crimes including enforced\ndisappearance and arbitrary detention. However, it notes that all human rights violations by military personnel\nshould fall under the civilian jurisdiction.\n1.4 National and State Human Rights Commissions and non-governmental human rights organizations\nThe Working Group met with the National Human Rights Commission and some state human rights bodies,\nas well as an array of non-governmental organizations which also conduct human rights work. It recalls that\nNational Human Rights Commission and the state-level human rights commissions must perform their\nfunctions in an autonomous and independent manner.\nThe Working Group welcomes the fact that the National Human Rights Commission has a legal basis under\nArticle 102 of the Constitution, in accordance with the Paris Principles on National Human Rights\nMechanisms. However, the Working Group was concerned to learn that visits to persons deprived of their\nliberty by members of the national and state human rights commissions did not involve follow-up to inform\nthem of efforts to redress their complaints.\n1 Vienna Convention on the Law of Treaties, Article 27.\n2 Report of the Working Group on Arbitrary Detention for 2007, A/HRC/7/4, para. 69. See also Principle 12 of the Body of Principles for the\nProtection of All Persons under Any Form of Detention or Imprisonment.\n2\n\nWith respect to non-governmental organizations, the Working Group was concerned to hear of specific\ninstances in which human rights defenders were subjected to reprisals, including through threats to fabricate\ncharges against them. These included members of civil society advocating for the rights of members of\nminorities such as the LGBTI+ community, indigenous persons, and afro-Mexicans. Such reprisals violate\ninter alia Article 12 of the Declaration on Human Rights Defenders.3\n2. Main Findings\nThe Working Group notes that arbitrary detention remains a widespread problem in Mexico due to a variety\nof causes and aggravating factors, both in the regulatory framework and in its implementation.\n2.1 Criminal Justice\n2.1.1. Mandatory Pre-trial Detention\nAlthough pre-trial detention should be regarded as an exceptional measure to ensure the availability of the\naccused during criminal proceedings, Article 19 of the Mexican Constitution sets out a list of felonies that\ntrigger mandatory pre-trial detention. In 2019, mandatory pre-trial detention was extended to 16 categories of\ncrimes, which are vaguely framed and susceptible to extension to an indeterminate range of offences. In 2021,\nmandatory pre-trial detention was introduced for other crimes.\nOf the approximately 90,000 people in pre-trial detention (“en proceso”) in 2022, around 50 percent are subject\nto mandatory pre-trial detention. Many of the detainees had been subjected to lengthy mandatory pre-trial\ndetention, including some who were still in mandatory pre-trial detention over 5 years after arrest. Worryingly,\nexcessively lengthy pre-trial detention was common amongst all detainees (noting that there was a total prison\npopulation of around 230,000 in Mexico in July 2023), not just those in mandatory pre-trial detention.\nThe Working Group is mindful that mandatory pre-trial detention has been identified as a violation of the right\nto personal liberty, as well as potentially undermining others, such as the presumption of innocence, the right\nto personal integrity and judicial independence. Mandatory pre-trial detention has been considered a violation\nof human rights in several opinions of the Working Group, including the Mexican context,4 and was declared\nto be a violation of Mexico’s obligations by the Inter-American Court of Human Rights, which ordered it to\nadjust its laws in this respect to meet international standards.\nThe Mexican authorities should reconcile its approach to preventive detention with international human rights\nlaw, by removing mandatory pre-trial detention from the Constitution and requiring that pre-trial detention\nmay only be applied based on an individualized assessment in which it is shown that the defendant is either a\nflight risk, at risk of serious re-offending, or at risk of tampering with evidence or witnesses.\n2.1.2. Arraigo (precautionary detention without charge)\nThe procedure known as arraigo was incorporated into the Constitution in 2008. It permits the authorities to\ndetain a person for up to 80 days for criminal investigation purposes in organized crime, without indicting that\nperson for a crime. The use of arraigo peaked between 2009 and 2012, but has decreased since 2013, and is\nrarely used these days. According to the information received by the Working Group, in 2022 it was used in\n25 cases. An attempt to remove arraigo from the Constitution in 2019 was unsuccessful.\nThe Inter-American Court of Human Rights and other international bodies have called for Mexico to eliminate\narraigo, as it violates the right to personal liberty and due process. Although authorities explained that arraigo\nis currently mainly used to protect witnesses in organised crimes cases, the Working Group notes that its\nongoing existence creates the risk of arbitrary detention. It encourages the Mexican authorities to remove the\nprocedure of arraigo from the Constitution and end its use as a basis for detention.\n3 UNGA, A/RES/53/144 8 March 1999 (“Declaration on the Right and Responsibility of Individuals, Groups and Organs of Society to Promote\nand Protect Universally Recognized Human Rights and Fundamental Freedoms”).\n4 See, e.g. Opinion Nos. 32/2023; 14/2021; 1/2018.\n3\n\n2.1.3. The militarization of public security\nIn Mexico, the use of military forces to undertake public policing activities at the federal, state and municipal\nlevel is frequent. There are reportedly over one hundred thousand soldiers currently carrying out policing and\npublic security tasks, as well as migratory control. This has been closely linked to rises in violence against\ndetainees, disappearances, and homicides. Detainees arrested by military personnel frequently were subjected\nto serious violence and torture, including beatings with foreign objects.\nSymptomatic of the extensive use of military elements in policing is the 2019 formation of the National Guard.\nAccording to the Constitution, the National Guard falls under the Ministry of Security and Citizen’s Protection.\nAlthough the incorporation of military personnel into the National Guard has a transitory nature, the armed\nforces have been authorised to continue public security functions until 2028.\nWhereas the National Guard took over the functions of the Federal Police, its personnel are predominantly\nmilitary by background. Of its 128,000 members, around 48 percent come from the Army and Navy.
