Although\nmembers of the National Guard are trained inter alia in human rights and in gathering evidence, the Working\nGroup notes that there are ongoing reports of violence in arrests and arbitrary detention by military personnel.\nAlthough arbitrary arrests committed by personnel of the Ministry of Defence and Navy have reportedly\ndecreased, 60 of 240 complaints of arbitrary detention from 2018-2023 remain unresolved.\n2.1.4. Excessive use of force during arrests\nDuring its country visit, the Working Group learned that a significant number of detainees suffer serious and\nexcessive violence during their arrests, such as a detainee being shot in the stomach by police, resulting parts\nof his intestine being removed. International human rights mechanisms, including the Working Group in its\nopinions, have also referred to ill-treatment and torture committed by security forces in Mexico.\nOften beatings and torture are inflicted to extract confessions. Detainees are sometimes forced to give\ninformation about other alleged suspects or potential evidence and ill-treatment is also used as a matter of\nhumiliation and punishment. For example, indigenous persons are sometimes beaten to force them to accept\nresponsibility and called “indio” during arrests. A woman who was pregnant at the time of arrest and informed\nofficers of this was hit in the stomach area and then driven around for hours by police to various locations to\nlook for evidence to incriminate her and her boyfriend. Others were “taken for a drive”, during which officers\nor military members did not deliver them promptly to the nearest civil authority, prosecutor, or judge, but\ninstead took them to remote locations and inflicted violence against them with a view to forcing them to\nprovide incriminating evidence.\nThese risks are exacerbated by the fact that a violative practice has emerged whereby the 48 hours within\nwhich a detainee must be brought before a judge is only counted beginning from when the detainee is presented\nto the prosecutor. This means that the time spent in police (or other security force) custody is not included in\nthe counting of the 48 hours, as was also accepted by authorities. Given that the initial period after arrest is a\ncritical time when a detainee is in most jeopardy of unduly self-incriminating or otherwise prejudicing their\nrights, it is critical that the 48 hours is counted from the time of the initial physical deprivation of liberty, rather\nthan from some later point in time. The Working Group also notes with concern that the 48 hr period is\nfrequently doubled to 96 hours in cases of organized crime. Moreover, the Working Group found deficiencies\nin the recording of apprehensions, with a variety of books, spreadsheets and other lists used across various\ninstitutions without a consistent methodology. Regarding accountability, although the authorities did refer to\ninstances of security agents being investigated for violence against detainees, they did not provide details of\nany significant number of convictions for such activities.\n2.1.5. Abuse of flagrante delicto\nMany arrests in Mexico are carried out without a pre-existing warrant, under the justification of flagrante\ndelicto. Since 2016, the rules governing the accusatory criminal process require that people detained in\nflagrante delicto without an arrest warrant must be brought before a judge to carry out a control of the\ndetention. However, the Working Group notes that expanded interpretations of flagrante delicto continue to\n4\n\nbe used. These arrests under expanded interpretations violate human rights, as set out in several opinions by\nthe Group on Arbitrary Detention.\nThe 2021 national survey of persons deprived of liberty indicated that 23 percent of detainees were arrested\nin the streets without an arrest warrant and that over 40 percent claimed that they were falsely accused of\ncommitting a crime. The Working Group recalls that, where a warrant is not obtained in advance, the scope of\njudicial control over detention is restricted to an ex post facto measure, by which time a violation may have\noccurred. There are circumstances, such as flagrante delicto or being \"caught in the act\", in which there is no\nopportunity to obtain a warrant ex ante. However, these must be interpreted restrictively to avoid undermining\njudicial control of detention.\nIn this respect, the Working Group was concerned to learn that an extended version of flagrante delicto, known\nas flagrante por señalamiento, or flagrancy by report, is applied in Mexico, whereby a person can be detained\nwithout a warrant at a later point in time despite not having been caught in the act by an official with policing\npowers, so long as a witness, who may be a private person, reports having seen them committing a crime.\nAnother extended application of this notion relates to the situation when a person who allegedly committed a\ncrime is not arrested immediately but is purportedly followed for a certain amount of time via street cameras,\nand then only arrested at a later stage. These extended interpretations of flagrante delicto are not consistent\nwith human rights law and must be prohibited.\n2.1.6. Deprivation of liberty under drug policy\nFrom 2006, Mexico has taken a far more punitive approach towards the use and dealing of drugs. The\npossession of drugs is still criminalised and even possession at relatively low levels can be sufficient to meet\nthe threshold to qualify as dealing drugs. As a result, there has been an increase in the imprisonment of drug\nusers. The Working Group emphasises the importance of looking for alternatives to detention in this context.\nConcerningly, the Working Group was informed of instances in which security forces fabricated charges\nagainst persons by placing drugs in their homes, vehicles, bags, or clothes. Reportedly, this practice continues\nto be rife among security forces. Allegations of such manufacturing of crimes must be taken extremely\nseriously, thoroughly investigated, and appropriately sanctioned where established. Where security forces can\nbe equipped with body-cams or other recording devices, this can assist to a degree in reducing the possibility\nfor such abuses of power. Additionally, the Working Group learned that detainees are often pre-textually\ncharged for specific offences only to have them changed to different crimes once they are detained, which can\nundermine their rights to liberty, against self-incrimination and to an effective defence.\nThe Amnesty Law includes crimes related to drug offending, provided that they fall under federal jurisdiction.\nIt covers vulnerable persons such as those in extreme poverty or indigenous people. However, the Working\nGroup was told that only around 320 people had been released via amnesty, as the parameters of the law have\nbeen restrictively interpreted and the Amnesty Committee has only met five times in three years. The Working\nGroup recalls that detaining a person despite there being an amnesty law applicable to them constitutes\narbitrary detention under Category I. It strongly encourages the Amnesty Committee to avoid an overly\nrestrictive approach to Amnesty Law, and to ensure that it implements amnesty-based releases expeditiously.\nOn a related note, the Working Group observed that photos of detainees posed in front of tables displaying\ndrugs and weapons often appear in the media, sometimes with the names of detainees or other identifying\ninformation. Such images have the potential to undermine the presumption of innocence in any subsequent\ncriminal proceedings. Although authorities have reportedly prohibited their own forces from disseminating\nsuch images, the posing of arrested detainees and illicit materials for the purpose of taking photos cannot be\ndone without at least the complicity of the arresting forces. Whilst media freedom must be respected, the\nWorking Group implores the Government of Mexico to ensure that its forces are not complicit in disseminating\nsuch images to the public prior to or during the criminal trials of any such detainees.\n2.1.7. Abbreviated trial procedure\nSince 2016, an abbreviated trial procedure has been incorporated into Mexican legislation by the reform of\nthe criminal process. Although the purpose is to reduce the strain on the criminal justice system, the Working\n5\n\nGroup learned that detainees were pressured, often by their own lawyers (frequently public defenders), to\naccept responsibility in order to avoid a trial and possible greater sanction. Whereas it should always be open\nto guilty persons to acknowledge culpability, and whereas this can expedite proceedings and avoid re-\ntraumatizing victims, caution must be taken to ensure that such abbreviated procedures are not used as a means\nof dissuading detainees from exercising their legitimate fair trial rights, including the right to silence, the\npresumption of innocence, and the right to adequate time and facilities to conduct their defence.\n2.1.8. Bureaucratisation of the judicial appeal (amparo trial-habeas corpus)\nThe procedure known as amparo, a judicial remedy available in Mexico, is similar to a habeas corpus writ. It\nis provided for in the Constitution and the Amparo Act of 2013. This writ can protect human rights, including\nto liberty and life. Under Mexican law, the writ of amparo is federal remedy, irrespective of whether a case\nconcerns crimes falling under a State entity’s jurisdiction (“común” crimes).\nWhile this procedure allows for release, the Working Group found out that this rarely occurs. For example, in\nCDMX, only 16.5% of cases in which arbitrary detention is found to have occurred result in the immediate\nrelease of the detainee. Similarly, the Committee on Enforced Disappearance noted, as a result of its country\nvisit in 2021, that few judges make use the amparo procedure (“amparo buscador”).5 As a matter of procedure,\nthe Working Group was assured that judges can order additional information from the prosecution office or\nother authorities in order to address amparo requests. However, of the detainees who exercised the right, the\nvast majority had seen their amparo rejected without any further inquiry. Others had seen their amparo requests\nremain undecided for years, including one case in which an individual had remained in detention for 9 years\nwithout trial. Of the few who had successful amparo proceedings, they had nonetheless not been immediately\nreleased but some remained in detention awaiting a re-trial or reactivation of charges which had been held in\nabeyance while they served their existing sentence.\nThe Working Group considers that the amparo procedure has considerable potential to ensure that human\nrights are respected and that individuals are released where violations have occurred. However, the Mexican\njudiciary must ensure that judges vigorously exercise their powers under the amparo procedure. For its part,\nthe Government and the judicial branch must ensure that all judges are aware of their powers to permit release\npursuant to amparo requests, and that they are not retaliated against for doing so.\n2.1.9. Judicial independence\nAn independent and impartial judiciary is essential to combatting arbitrary detention, as reflected inter alia by\nArticle 14(1) of the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights. The Working Group welcomes\npositive changes in this respect introduced with the reform of the criminal justice system. It was however\nconcerned to learn that members of the executive authorities have pressured judges, particularly when those\njudges have ruled that executive initiatives contravene the law. In some cases, executive members name\nindividual judges who issued decisions or opinions impugning government policies. More broadly, the federal\njudiciary is facing scathing budgetary cuts, reportedly up to 20 percent. Such measures undermine the ability\nof the judiciary to independently conduct its work without fear nor favour. The Working Group is concerned\nthat this has contributed to the ongoing persistence of high numbers of cases of arbitrary detention at both the\nfederal and state levels.\n2.1.10 Right to legal assistance\nThe Working Group welcomes the information that approximately 95 percent of individuals charged with\nfelonies are assigned public defenders to represent them free of charge. It also appreciates the efforts to provide\na wide range of important legal services, including legal assistance to migrants. Additionally, it notes that the\ntransition to an accusatory system has placed public defence lawyers on a more equal footing with the\nprosecution than previously. However, public defenders face heavy caseloads which compromise the quality\nof their services. Many detainees receive poor quality legal services. Moreover, the caseload results in a\nsignificant burden for the Public Defender’s Offices (PDOs), which has financial and staffing constraints.\n5 Para.59.\n6\n\nThe result is a heightened risk of arbitrary detention particularly for persons lacking socio-economic means to\nobtain private legal assistance. Additionally, many detainees lack regular access to public defence counsel\nonce detained; they are unable to contact lawyers by phone, as reportedly such phone calls are often not\nfacilitated free of charge and public defenders often ignore calls from a detention facility. The Working Group\nreiterates its call to strengthen the PDOs (building on its recommendation (d) of its 2002 country visit report).\nIt calls on the authorities to improve mechanisms for access to legal assistance (including through public\ndefenders), particularly for detained persons, in a regular and timely manner.\n2.2 Protections in detention\n2.2.1. Conditions of detention\nSafeguards for detained persons help to prevent arbitrary detention. Under human rights law, safeguards\ninclude facilitating prompt and continuing access to a defence lawyer, conducting a post-arrest medical\nexamination, and communications with third parties as well as visits by external oversight mechanisms.\nThe Working Group learned that these safeguards vary from one facility to another and are frequently not fully\nobserved by the authorities in Mexico. Detainees struggled to obtain access to a lawyer once detained. Many\ndetainees were only allowed one ten-minute phone call per 8 days, which they could use either to talk to their\nfamily or lawyer. Given that they usually choose to talk to family and given that lawyers rarely visit the\ndetention facilities which are often located in remote areas, this restriction has considerable potential to\nprejudice pre-trial detainees' exercise of fair trial rights.\nIll-treatment and violence during detention can also impact on a detainee’s ability to exercise their rights,\nparticularly if they suffer injuries as a result thereof. At the federal level, many detainees suffered excessive\nviolence during their arrests but that the Working Group heard that penitentiary staff generally did not use\nviolence against them. However, there were instances of violence by prison guards, indicating that this\nviolative practice has not been eliminated. There is also corruption in some facilities. Moreover, pre-trial\ndetainees (not subject to any conviction) were often housed together with convicted detainees. Several\ndetainees had serious wounds and illnesses and complained that they were unable to access effective medical\ntreatment. A particularly prevalent concern was the lack of medication available to unwell detainees. Many\ncould only get medication if their families obtained it for them. The Working Group emphasizes that where it\nis medically necessary, the authorities must ensure its availability to detainees. Detainees also complained\nabout improper food and the Working Group observed overcrowded conditions in some facilities, with\ndetainees sometimes sleeping on concrete floors without blankets being provided, which are not compatible\nwith human rights protections.\nIn some prisons at the federal and state levels, detainees are kept locked in their cells most of the day and\nrarely, if ever, leave the cell block in which they were housed. Prisons are hampered by chronic staff shortages,\nincluding in key roles such as security guards and prison medical staff. Those shortages impact on their ability\nto take detainees out of their cell blocks for exercise and activities. This was particularly noticeable at the\nmen's federal facilities visited, where many detainees had spent months without leaving their closed-in cell\nblocks.\nRegarding sanctions in detention, the Working Group noted that detainees found to violate prison rules were\noften placed in deplorable cells, with no bedding and only hard concrete floors to sleep on. Such conditions\nare not compatible with human rights guarantees as reflected in the Nelson Mandela Rules. Moreover, solitary\nconfinement was available for use as a punishment for up to 15 days for misconduct. In reality, however, the\nWorking Group found out that confinement for 30 days and more continues, albeit usually in small groups.\nThe Working Group recalls that Rule 45 of the Nelson Mandela Rules provides that solitary confinement shall\nbe used only in exceptional cases as a last resort, for as short a time as possible and subject to independent\nreview, and only pursuant to the authorization by a competent authority. It should be prohibited in the case of\nprisoners with psycho-social disabilities or physical disabilities.\nA systemic safeguard for detainees is prison registries. However, the Working Group found that registries in\nprisons were non-effective, as they were not consistently utilised and updated by the authorities. There were\n7\n\nconcerns raised about poor internet connectivity, old equipment, and a lack of trained human resources to\nupdate the registry. These shortcomings must be addressed to ensure this tool is available.\nWhile some prisons were under capacity, the Working Group also discovered that severe over-crowding in\nsome male prisons visited by the Group is of particular concern. In one of them, for example, 16 pre-trial\ndetainees are kept locked in small rooms. The Working Group calls upon the authorities to set minimum\nrequirements in respect of the floor space and cubic content of air, per inmate to respect human rights including\nthe right to adequate time and facilities to prepare a defence. It also reiterates the link between overcrowding\nand an excessive length of pre-trial detention, which has been found by this Group to violate the ICCPR, and\nencourages the authorities to resort to alternative preventive measures.\n2.3 People in vulnerable situations\n2.3.1. Migrants\nThe Working Group notes that Mexico is a transitory step for migrants seeking to access the United States of\nAmerica from Central and South America as well as a growing array of other states. Many of those people are\nasylum seekers. In recent years, the situation in Central America, Ecuador, and Venezuela has led to a dramatic\nincrease in the numbers of migrants passing through Mexico, or in some cases using it as destination country.\nFlamed by the migration policies of the United States of America, the situation has resulted in migrants often\nbeing subjected to conditions in contravention of Mexico's human rights commitments.\nLarge numbers of migrants and asylum seekers are detained in Mexico, amounting to over 240,000 in the first\nhalf of 2023. Under the Constitution, such administrative detentions should be limited to 36 hours and the\nSupreme Court has held that detention of migrants for 15-60 days under the Migration Law is unconstitutional.\nHowever, the Working Group discovered that a significant number of detained migrants are held beyond the\n36-hour time limit, which raises the risk of arbitrary detention. Moreover, the Working Group learned that\nwhen migrants who are detained for crimes seek to exercise legal rights, such as amparo, then they are often\ntold they will be kept for months instead of being let go in 36 hours. There should be no punishment for\nexercising legal rights, particularly in context of migration.\nA large number of children are detained in the context of migration. In 2022 alone, over 126,000 were\nchannelled into centres run by the National Agency for Family Development (“DIF”). Under Article 99 of the\nMigration Act, children are not permitted to be held in migrant detention. However, in practice the Working\nGroup found that children migrants were typically deprived of liberty, either in shelters solely for\nunaccompanied minors run by DIF or in facilities co-located with migrant facilities under the authority of the\nInstitute for Migration of the Ministry of the Interior.\nHaving visited children deprived of liberty under the authority of the DIF, the Working Group noted that,\nalthough they were fed and sheltered, it is important that the Mexican authorities provide them with sufficient\nopportunities to ensure their development is not harmed and to ensure that any such detention is exceptional,\nwhen alternatives to detention are manifestly unfeasible, and that any such detention is limited in time.\nHaving also visited children deprived of liberty with family members in facilities co-located with migration\ndetention facilities, the Working Group was concerned at the conditions. Children and their family members\nwere sleeping in courtyard areas outside the facility buildings, in the heat. Moreover, whilst law reforms had\nreportedly prohibited the detention of child migrants, the Working Group saw children who were manifestly\nbelow 18 (and in some cases clearly below 10 years of age) deprived of liberty in these areas co-located with\nmigration detention facilities.\nInside the migrant detention facility buildings themselves, the detained persons were not free to circulate\nwithin the facilities. Concerningly, the Working Group saw that metal gates with locks separated the rooms\nwhere migrants were housed from exits to the outside courtyards and guards prevented migrants from freely\naccessing those open-air locations. This also prevented migrants from accessing the complaints boxes of the\nrelevant human rights commissions. Given the fire at the Ciudad Juárez facilities in March 2023, in which 40\n8\n\npeople died and many were injured, the Working Group emphasizes that migrants should not be kept behind\nlocked doors and should have access to outdoor areas freely.\nMigrants also are subjected to frequent extortion of bribes by Mexican officials, often from the security forces.\nThose unable to pay were consequently detained, whereas others who were able to pay were allowed to go on\ntheir way. The widespread occurrence of such extortion risks creating an underground economy, which may\nbecome entrenched if security forces grow accustomed to extracting additional income in this unlawful\nmanner. Any such extortion must be prohibited and stamped out in law and in practice.\n2.3.2 Adolescents\nThe detention of adolescents from age 12 up to 17 in Mexico is subject to a special legal regime. Adolescents\nwho are detained are kept in separate facilities from adults. While the facilities were largely clean and the\nWorking Group did not learn of violence by staff or any other shortcomings within the course of the\nproceedings against them or in the detention, opportunities for external activities, such as visits to sporting\nand cultural events, were relatively limited and only available to small groups among the youths in the\nfacilities. Given the developmental stage of their lives, it is important that all the detained youths should have\nopportunities to participate in stimulating activities.\nMore concerningly, many adolescents experience considerable violence in their initial arrests and are not\nnotified of the reasons for the arrest. This is reflected in a national survey which revealed that 66 percent of\nyouths complained of suffering some kind of violent attack during their arrests. Youths were sometimes beaten\nby arresting forces who placed bags over their heads, and taken for drives during which they were pressured\nto confess to crimes. Moreover, adolescents are sometimes told by the defence lawyers to plead guilty to the\ncrimes in order to avoid going into the adult criminal justice system. The Working Group emphasizes the need\nfor adolescents to be given access to appropriately trained lawyers who rigorously defend their interests, given\nthe vulnerable age they are at and the detrimental impact that being incarcerated will typically have on their\nfuture lives and physical and psychological well-being.\n2.3.3 Indigenous people\nThe Working Group learned of instances in which indigenous people faced discrimination leading to their\narrest and subjection to considerable violence by security forces. These included cases where the indigenous\npersons were advocating for the rights of their people, including on governmental premises and projects, and\nwere charged with trespass and criminal damage. In this respect, the Working Group emphasizes that persons\nshould not be subjected to detention merely due to exercising their rights, and that this is particularly important\nfor indigenous groups engaging in non-violent protest actions. The Working Group notes that many indigenous\npersons advocate to protect the environment, and thus have a dual-faceted role, seeking to uphold the rights\nof their fellow indigenous people while also defending nature. Such activities should not be met by criminal\nsanction unless they involve serious violent offending or other grave crimes which cannot be addressed\nthrough alternative means. At the same time, if indigenous groups dispense traditional justice involving\ndetention, this must accord with Mexico’s human rights obligations including the prohibition of arbitrary\ndetention and the prohibition of torture, cruel, inhuman, and degrading treatment.\nFor indigenous persons in detention, which constitutes a significant proportion of detainees particularly in\nChiapas, the Working Group learned that culturally appropriate food was not consistently available and that\nthey were not consistently able to observe traditional practices. Regarding language, the Working Group notes\nthe efforts of the National Institute of Indigenous Peoples, which assisted over 500,000 people in 2022 and an\nincreasing number in 2023. This is important from the outset of deprivation of liberty, when the jeopardy of\nundermining legal rights is highest. The Working Group encourages the authorities to be proactive in training\nprosecutors, judges and other officials in the needs of indigenous persons in detention, which extend beyond\nlanguage and also encompass socio-economic challenges, remoteness, and alienation from their peoples.\n9\n\n2.3.4 Detention of persons with psycho-social disabilities\nAccording to the Ministry of Health, approximately 24 million persons in Mexico have psycho-social\ndisabilities, 3.5 million of which have chronic symptoms that seriously impact their lives. Many of these\npersons face detention. The Working Group emphasizes that persons with psycho-social disabilities should be\naddressed by the authorities under the framework of health treatment and consistently with the right to liberty.6\nUntil recently, individuals deemed incompetent to participate in criminal proceedings were nonetheless able\nto be sanctioned for crimes through detention for a period corresponding to the crime. In 2019, the General\nHealth Law was amended to introduce a new model for the treatment of those with psychiatric and/or\nsubstance abuse disorders and place more emphasis on the patients’ autonomy and freedom of choice of\ntreatment. If individuals are diagnosed as having psycho-social disabilities that preclude their fitness to stand\ntrial for a crime, they can be detained via an involuntary internment as a safety measure, and the period of\ninternment relates to the disorder of the individual rather than the nature of the crime committed.\nDespite these changes, the Working Group was concerned to discover that individuals with serious psycho-\nsocial disabilities detained in penitentiaries, along with persons convicted for serious crimes. Although those\nwith disorders were sometimes housed in separate blocks, they were otherwise subject to the same carceral\nconditions as the general population. Only negligible psycho-social disabilities treatment was available, with\nno psychiatrists among the prisons’ medical staff on site. It also emerged that persons deemed incapable of\nparticipating in trial proceedings are detained at prison facilities in some cases. The Working Group\nemphasizes that persons with serious psycho-social disabilities which preclude their competence to be tried\nunder criminal law should never be detained at criminal penitentiaries. Moreover, any person detained,\nincluding persons detained under the “safe-guarding” mechanism for their personal health, should be\nsystematically recorded in the National Register of Detentions, with appropriate care paid to protect their\nprivate personal data and health-related information.\n2.3.5 LGBTI+ persons\nWith respect to LGBTI+ persons, they were generally housed together to insulate against any potential\nharassment. They were reportedly not subjected to any notably different treatment to other detainees or other\ndiscrimination. However, prison officials noted that there were no particular protocols on how to address the\nparticular needs of members of this community in detention. The Working Group considers that the authorities\nshould undertake consultations to determine whether specific protocols are required beyond the existing laws\nand to identify best practices which could be followed.\n3. Implementation and follow-up of the Working Group’s opinions\nDuring the visit, the Working Group met multiple individuals who had been released from detention as a result\nof being subjects of its opinions. This step demonstrates a commitment of the authorities to improving rights\nprotection and compliance with the international human rights norms. However, others whom the Working\nGroup has found to be arbitrarily detained remain in detention. The Working Group calls on the Mexican\nauthorities to systematically implement all opinions which find that arbitrary detention has occurred. The\nGroup further encourages to close gaps in domestic accountability by investigating cases of arbitrary detention\nand paying compensation to the victims, as well as following other recommendations set up in its opinions. It\ncalls on the authorities to conduct public outreach campaigns to raise public awareness of human rights,\nincluding the prohibition of arbitrary detention.\nConclusion\nThese are the preliminary findings of the Working Group. We look forward to continue engaging in this\nconstructive dialogue with the Government of Mexico while we determine our final conclusions in relation to\nthis country visit. We acknowledge with gratitude the willingness and openness of the Government to invite\nthe Working Group and note that this is an opportunity for introducing reforms to address situations, which\nmay amount to arbitrary deprivation of liberty.\n6 United Nations Basic Principles and Guidelines on Remedies and Procedures on the Right of Anyone Deprived of Their Liberty to Bring\nProceedings Before a Court, Guideline 20.\n10", "excerpt_sha256": "4df49829d888ef942f495010fa20a2093ad5389bd866afccd1e52a51735e5f04", "verification_status": "corrected", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt specifically describes child detention as contrary to legal restrictions and migrant detention beyond stated limits; 'unlawful migrant and child detention' was narrowed."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0050-E010", "source_id": "SRC-57361184a7547261", "url": "https://apnews.com/article/42841e8c9ef6a1e448f27a72dfc39c60", "title": "Two hours of terror and now years of devastation for Acapulco's poor in Hurricane Otis aftermath", "publisher": "Associated Press", "raw_sha256": "962463be3101732a30f472030556009ba174d6e906e7c4a178274c9f40ed7cac", "fact_summary": "After Hurricane Otis, at least 45 people were reported dead, nearly 220,000 homes damaged, and 47 people missing. It assessed the “abrazos, no balazos” policy as associated with expanding criminal control, reduced security spending and reduced U.S. security and counternarcotics cooperation.", "question_ids": ["2B.10", "3B.10", "3B.6", "3B.9", "8B.7", "8B.9"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "Published in 2023 and evaluates outcomes during the presidency through 2022 and early 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The security strategy and policy choices were attributed to López Obrador.", "limitations": ["Security outcomes are affected by longstanding criminal fragmentation and external factors; the report presents an analytical causal assessment."], "start_unit": 17, "end_unit": 18, "locator": "page 17 through page 18", "exact_excerpt": "Security\nMexico’s economic growth and opportunities are inextricably tied to its security environment.59 Here\nthe prognosis under AMLO and beyond 2024 appears bleak. Each year, Mexico experiences around\n30,000 murders, which have risen to record levels during AMLO’s mandate.60 In 2021 the nation\nregistered 34,000 homicides, double the number in 2015, while 2022 was the most violent year ever recorded\nby the National Public Security System.61 According to Bloomberg, Mexico remains home to eight of the world’s\nmost dangerous cities.62 Insecurity also characterizes the political scene: in the 2021 midterm elections, 100\npolitical assassinations were reported, 35 of which targeted political candidates.63 With a year and a half\nremaining in AMLO’s term, homicides on his watch have already equaled those under his predecessor’s\nsix-year term and have surpassed by 22 percent the total during the six-year term of Felipe Calderón, the\npresident whose war on drugs AMLO has derided. Simply put, AMLO’s sexenio will be the bloodiest six-year\nterm in Mexican history.64\nViolence associated with conflict against drug cartels claims most of these victims. López Obrador’s “abrazos,\nno balazos” (hugs, not bullets) security policy has seen criminal organizations expand their control to claim up\nto 40 percent of Mexican territory.65 With Mexico experiencing some of the highest levels of extortion, human\ntrafficking, human rights violations, and femicides registered in the country’s history, Mexico’s situation under\nAMLO displays an alarming trend.66 These patterns present no signs of slowing, as AMLO’s security reforms\nhave reduced public spending on security, muddied the citizen security landscape, and reduced cooperation\nwith the United States on security and counternarcotics issues.67\n13 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024\n\nMembers of the Mexican army drive on a road a day after an operation to arrest the son of Joaquín “El Chapo”\nGuzmán, Ovidio Guzmán, in Culiacán, Sinaloa state, Mexico, on January 6, 2023.\nPhoto Source: Juan Carlos Cruz/AFP/Getty Imges\nOrganized Crime\nAmong the many challenges Mexico faces, organized crime remains one of the most complex and resilient\nthreats the country has yet to find a successful strategy for addressing. In 2015 there were around 25 cartels\nrecognized as major criminal groupings—or what the U.S. Department of Justice calls “well-articulated”\ncriminal groups. By 2019 this figure had grown to 37, which is likely a significant underestimate.68 In fact,\nindependent observers identified the presence of up to 463 criminal entities on Mexican territory, explaining\nthat criminal groupings have grown ever smaller following internal fragmentation and the intensification\nof violence as a result of the government’s targeting of kingpins.69 At the same time, an underworld\nso fragmented and difficult to trace makes it even harder for political leadership to find adequate and\neffective measures to counter its activity, resulting in a vicious cycle. Some of these groups, such as Jalisco\nNew Generation Cartel (CJNG) or the Sinaloa Cartel, are powerful enough to challenge the state, and the\ntransformation and fragmentation they provoke either within or between each other often causes deadly\nepisodes of violence.70 Indeed, U.S. Drug Enforcement Administration (DEA) administrator Anne Milgram has\nstated that these two groups pose “the greatest criminal threat” ever faced by the United States.71\nDuring the pandemic, the role of criminal groups became twofold. On the one hand, they exacerbated insecurity\nas violent attacks on civilians and battles with other groups continued apace. On the other, criminal groups\ncomplemented, or in some cases took on, the role of the state by distributing food and aid and imposing a curfew\nto limit the spread of Covid-19. This was the case for the Sinaloa Cartel, which distributed “Chapo dispensas,”\nor boxes of supplies with erstwhile cartel leader Joaquín “El Chapo” Guzmán’s face emblazoned on them.72\nGovernment restrictions on mobility and tighter border controls also affected their activity, and as a result new\nchannels of criminality arose, such as cybercrimes, which are harder for authorities to detect.73 High profits\nand low risks for activities such as fraud, piracy, and cybertheft quickly attracted drug cartels and generated\n14 | Ryan C. "Pleno\nadvierte\nque,\nindependientemente\nde\nlos\ncambios\norgánicos,\nde\nmando\ny\nde\nadscripción\nrealizados\na\nla\nGuardia\nNacional,\nel\nDecreto\nimpugnado\nadicionó,\na\ntravés\nde\n\nlos\nartículos\n7,\nfracción\nIX,\n17,\n18,\n19\ny\n32\nBis\nde\nla\nLey\nde\nla\nGuardia\nNacional\ny\nartículo\n2\nBis\nde\nla\nLey\n\nOrgánica\ndel\nEjército\ny\nFuerza\nAérea\nMexicanos,\nnormas\ninstrumentales\npara\nla\ncolaboración\nentre\nla\nGuardia\nNacional\ny\nla\nFuerza\nArmada\npermanente,\nrespecto\nde\nla\nseguridad\n\npública\n,\ny\nque\nsubsiste,\nrespecto\nde\nlas\nmismas,\nuna\nparte\nde\nla\ncuestión\nplanteada\npor\nla\naccionante\n:\n¿es\n\"\nválido\nconforme\na\nla\n\nregularidad\nconstitucional\nordinaria\nque\nla\nFuerza\nArmada\npermanente\nse\ninvolucre\nen\ncuestiones\nde\nseguridad\npública\n\"\n?\n(183)\n349.\nEn\nconcreto,\nel\nDecreto\nimpugnado\nestableció\n\nla\nobligación\npara\nel\nComisario\nGeneral,\npara\nel\nComisario\nJefe\ny\npara\nla\npersona\ntitular\ndel\nBatallón,\nde\ncada\nCoordinación\nTerritorial,\nEstatal\ny\nde\nUnidad,\n\nrespectivamente,\nde\nmantener\nun\nenlace\ncon\nsus\nhomólogos\nde\nlas\nFuerzas\nArmadas,\n\"\na\nfin\nde\nfacilitar\nuna\nadecuada\ncolaboración\npara\nel\ndesempeño\nde\nlas\n\nfunciones\nde\nseguridad\npública\n\"\n.\n(184)\nPara\neste\nefecto,\nen\nel\nartículo\n32\nBis\nde\nla\nLGN\n,\nel\nlegislador\nprevió\ncuál\nsería\nla\n\nequivalencia\njerárquica\nentre\nel\npersonal\nde\nla\nGuardia\nNacional\ny\nel\nde\nlas\nFuerzas\nArmadas.\n(185)\nEn\nrelación\ncon\nlo\nanterior,\nel\nDecreto\nimpugnado\nadicionó\n\nun\nartículo\n2\nBis\na\nla\nLOEFAM\npara\nestablecer\nque\nel\npersonal\nmilitar\npodrá\nefectuar\noperaciones\nde\napoyo\na\nlas\ninstituciones\nde\nseguridad\npública\nen\n\nlos\ntérminos\nque\nseñale\nel\nmarco\njurídico\naplicable.\n(186)\n350.\nEl\nDecreto\nimpugnado\ntambién\nadicionó\nuna\nfracción\nIX\nal\nartículo\n7\nde\nla\nLGN\npara\n\nincorporar,\ncomo\nuno\nde\nlos\nfines\nde Justicia de la Nación / Diario Oficial", "raw_sha256": "98a7d3266296fedf19483441f4f02d0ada90b5c768db37da9c85a9b031f5d3fc", "fact_summary": "The Court held that the Guardia Nacional disciplinary framework included complaint escalation, written and reasoned disciplinary orders, proportionality criteria, presumption of innocence, public hearings, defense counsel, evidence and arguments, recusals, and an appeal mechanism through the regulation.", "question_ids": ["3B.2", "3B.4", "8B.2"], "polarity": "favorable", "period_fit": "These procedural safeguards were recognized as applicable 309 through extracted HTML block 325", "exact_excerpt": "Acciones de inconstitucionalidad: 61/2016; 107/2016; 73/2018; 85/2018; 86/2018; 50/\n2019; 106/2019; 111/2019; 108/2020; 115/2020; 117/2020; 118/2020; 182/2020; 184/\n2020; 192/2020; 263/2020; y 56/2021.\n·\nContradicción de tesis 448/2016.\n·\nAmparo en revisión 548/2018.\n·\nCorte Interamericana de Derechos Humanos,\nCaso Chaparro Álvarez y Lapo Íñiguez vs.\nEcuador.\nVI.5. Estudio relativo a la omisión de regular un procedimiento para la imposición de sanciones\ndisciplinarias a los miembros de la Guardia Nacional.\n500.\nEste Tribunal Pleno considera que el Congreso de la Unión no fue omiso en regular el procedimiento\npara la imposición de correcciones y sanciones disciplinarias aplicables a los miembros de la Guardia\nNacional. Primero, porque no existe un mandato constitucional para ello. Segundo, porque dentro de las\nnormas controvertidas se encuentra el derecho de audiencia que puede utilizarse en el régimen de\ndisciplina y, la propia Ley de la Guardia Nacional prevé válidamente mecanismos de remisión a su\nreglamento en los que está previsto el ejercicio del derecho de audiencia.\n501.\nEsencia del concepto de invalidez quinto.\nLa CNDH alegó, en resumen, que los artículos 57 a 71 de\nla Ley de la Guardia Nacional, esto es, los capítulos I\n(219)\n\ny II\n(220)\ndel título Quinto de la Ley\n(221)\n, son\ninconstitucionales, pues el Congreso de la Unión no reguló lo necesario para que los miembros de la\nGuardia Nacional puedan defenderse ante los Consejos de Disciplina en los casos en los que se les\npueda imponer alguna responsabilidad de índole disciplinaria.\n502.\nEn otras palabras, de acuerdo con la Comisión actora, los preceptos impugnados vulneran el debido\nproceso al resultar violatorios del derecho de audiencia previa previsto en el párrafo segundo del artículo\n14 Constitucional.\n503.\nA su vez, la actora solicita a este Tribunal Constitucional que, en este punto, se tengan por reproducidos\ncomo si a la letra se insertasen, todos los razonamientos vertidos en el primer concepto de invalidez en\nrelación con las omisiones legislativas parciales o relativas de carácter obligatorio. Lo anterior, con\nrelación a que el Congreso de la Unión omitió consagrar en los capítulos referidos de la Ley, las\nformalidades esenciales del procedimiento.\n504.\nMetodología para atender el concepto de invalidez.\nEste Tribunal Pleno considera, atendiendo a la\ncuestión efectivamente planteada\n(222)\n, que el concepto de invalidez resulta\ninfundado\n\n. Por lo que es\nnecesario establecer el contenido de las normas controvertidas. Luego dar razones para sustentar por\nqué no estamos ante un caso de omisión legislativa, para después desarrollar consideraciones sobre las\ncaracterísticas de las correcciones disciplinarias y la manera en que se ejerce el derecho de audiencia,\npara concluir que en esos casos sí existe una norma que establece ese derecho, así como una remisión\nal reglamento de la Guardia Nacional que estable el procedimiento disciplinario.\n505.\nArtículos controvertidos.\nRégimen Disciplinario\nCapítulo I\nDe las Responsabilidades y Procedimientos Sancionatorios\nArtículo 57. El personal de la Guardia Nacional deberá sujetar su conducta a la observancia de\nlas leyes, órdenes y jerarquías, a la obediencia a la superioridad, así como al Código de Ética\nde la Guardia Nacional.\nArtículo 58. El personal de la Guardia Nacional que tenga alguna queja en relación con las\nórdenes de sus superiores o las obligaciones que le imponga el servicio, podrá acudir ante el\nsuperior inmediato para la solución de sus demandas y, en caso de no ser debidamente\natendido, podrá recurrir, por rigurosa escala, hasta el Comandante, si es necesario.\nArtículo 59. El personal de la Guardia Nacional que infrinja la presente Ley, así como algún\nprecepto reglamentario, se hará acreedor a un correctivo o sanción disciplinaria, de acuerdo\ncon su jerarquía.\nSi la falta también conlleva la posible comisión de un delito, quedará sujeto a las disposiciones\naplicables.\nArtículo 60. Son deberes del personal de la Guardia Nacional:\nI. Conducir su actuación con dedicación y disciplina, así como con apego al orden jurídico y\n\nrespeto a los derechos humanos reconocidos en la Constitución Política de los Estados Unidos\nMexicanos, y los Tratados Internacionales de la materia de los que el Estado Mexicano\nsea\nparte;\nII. Preservar la secrecía, reserva o confidencialidad de los asuntos que conozcan por razón del\ndesempeño de su función;\nIII. Prestar auxilio a las personas amenazadas por algún peligro o que hayan sido víctimas u\nofendidos por algún delito, así como brindar protección a sus bienes y derechos. Su actuación\nserá congruente, oportuna y proporcional al hecho;\nIV. Utilizar el uniforme y las insignias de la institución policial que les correspondan, y cumplir\nsus funciones con absoluta imparcialidad y sin discriminación alguna;\nV. Abstenerse de infligir o tolerar actos de tortura, tratos crueles, inhumanos o degradantes, así\ncomo desaparición forzada, aun cuando se trate de una orden superior o se argumenten\ncircunstancias especiales, tales como amenaza a la seguridad pública o urgencia de las\ninvestigaciones. Cuando tenga conocimiento de ello, deberá denunciarlo inmediatamente a la\nautoridad competente;\nVI. Observar un trato respetuoso con todas las personas, debiendo abstenerse de todo acto\narbitrario;\nVII. Desempeñar su misión sin solicitar ni aceptar compensaciones, pagos o gratificaciones\ndistintas a las previstas legalmente. En particular, se opondrá a cualquier acto de corrupción y,\nen caso de tener conocimiento de alguno, deberá denunciarlo;\nVIII. Abstenerse de ordenar o realizar la detención de persona alguna sin cumplir con los\nrequisitos previstos en los ordenamientos constitucionales y legales aplicables;\nIX. Velar por la vida e integridad física de las personas detenidas;\nX. Actualizarse en el empleo de métodos de investigación que garanticen la recopilación técnica\n\ny científica de evidencias para el ejercicio de sus funciones;\nXI. Utilizar los protocolos de investigación y de cadena de custodia que establezcan las\nautoridades competentes;\nXII. Participar en operativos de coordinación con otras instituciones de seguridad pública, así\ncomo brindarles, en su caso, el apoyo que conforme a derecho proceda;\nXIII. Preservar, conforme a las disposiciones aplicables, las pruebas e indicios de probables\nhechos delictivos o de infracciones administrativas, de forma que no pierdan su calidad\nprobatoria y se facilite la correcta tramitación del procedimiento correspondiente;\nXIV. Abstenerse de disponer de los bienes asegurados para beneficio propio o de terceros;\nXV. Someterse a evaluaciones periódicas para acreditar el cumplimiento de los requisitos de\npermanencia, así como obtener y mantener vigente la certificación respectiva;\nXVI. Informar al superior jerárquico, de manera inmediata, las omisiones, infracciones o delitos\nde los que tenga conocimiento;\nXVII. Cumplir y hacer cumplir con diligencia las órdenes que reciba con motivo del desempeño\nde sus funciones, evitando todo acto u omisión que produzca deficiencia en su cumplimiento;\nXVIII. Fomentar la disciplina, responsabilidad, decisión, integridad, espíritu de cuerpo y\nprofesionalismo, en sí mismo y en el personal bajo su mando;\nXIX. Inscribir las detenciones en el Registro Nacional de Detenciones, conforme a las\ndisposiciones aplicables;\nXX. Abstenerse de sustraer, ocultar, alterar o dañar información o bienes en perjuicio de las\ninstituciones;\nXXI. Abstenerse, conforme a las disposiciones aplicables, de dar a conocer, por cualquier\nmedio a quien no tenga derecho, documentos, registros, imágenes, constancias, estadísticas,\nreportes o cualquier otra información reservada o confidencial de la que tenga conocimiento, en\nejercicio y con motivo de su empleo, cargo o comisión;\n\nXXII. Atender con diligencia la solicitud de información, queja o auxilio de la ciudadanía o de\nsus propios subordinados, excepto cuando la petición rebase su ámbito de competencia, en\ncuyo caso deberá turnarlo a la autoridad o servidor público que corresponda;\nXXIII. Abstenerse de introducir a las instalaciones de la Guardia Nacional bebidas\nembriagantes, sustancias psicotrópicas, estupefacientes u otras sustancias adictivas de\ncarácter ilegal, prohibido o controlado, salvo cuando sean producto de detenciones, cateos,\naseguramientos u otros similares, y previamente exista la autorización correspondiente;\nXXIV. Abstenerse de consumir, dentro o fuera del servicio, sustancias psicotrópicas,\nestupefacientes u otras sustancias adictivas de carácter ilegal o prohibido. El consumo de\nmedicamentos controlados deberá realizarse mediante prescripción médica, avalada y\ncertificada por el servicio médico de la Guardia Nacional;\nXXV. Abstenerse de consumir en las instalaciones de la Guardia Nacional o en actos del\nservicio, bebidas embriagantes; así como de presentarse a sus labores bajo sus efectos;\nXXVI. Abstenerse de realizar conductas que desacrediten su persona o la imagen de la Guardia\nNacional, dentro o fuera del servicio;\nXXVII. Impedir que personas ajenas a la Guardia Nacional realicen actos inherentes a las\natribuciones que tenga encomendadas o que le acompañen durante la realización de actos del\nservicio;\nXXVIII. Abstenerse de asistir uniformado a bares, cantinas, centros de apuestas y juegos, o\nprostíbulos u otros lugares de este tipo, si no media orden expresa para el desempeño de\nfunciones o en casos de flagrancia;\nXXIX. Hacer uso de la fuerza de manera racional y proporcional, conforme a lo previsto en la\nley en la materia;\nXXX. Comportarse con el más alto grado de cortesía y educación, guardando la compostura\nque corresponde a su dignidad como servidor público;\n\nXXXI. Prestar, siempre que le sea posible, su ayuda moral y material a sus subordinados y\ncompañeros que la necesiten;\nXXXII. Abstenerse de dar órdenes cuya ejecución constituya un delito; el personal de la Guardia\nNacional que las emita y el subordinado que las cumpla, serán responsables conforme a la\nlegislación penal aplicable;\nXXXIII. Abstenerse de abandonar el país sin autorización del superior facultado para ello;\nXXXIV. Abstenerse de abandonar, sin autorización del superior facultado para ello, la entidad\nfederativa a la que está adscrito o en donde deba permanecer;\nXXXV. Mantener respeto a sus superiores jerárquicos, acatar y ejecutar sus órdenes, salvo que\natenten contra la ley y los derechos humanos;\nXXXVI. Abstenerse de dar órdenes de índole personal o que no tengan relación con el servicio\no para impedir la ejecución de los deberes o facultades del subordinado;\nXXXVII. Abstenerse de obstaculizar algún medio de defensa o petición que quiera hacer valer\nun subordinado, insultarlo o inducirlo a cometer una acción degradante, una infracción o un\ndelito;\nXXXVIII. Aplicar los correctivos o sanciones disciplinarios que correspondan, de manera\nproporcional a la falta cometida, y\nXXXIX. Los demás que establezca la presente Ley.\nEl incumplimiento de los deberes contenidos en las fracciones XXXIII a la XXXVIII serán\nconsideradas faltas graves a la disciplina y podrán ser sancionadas con suspensión o\nremoción.\nArtículo 61. La disciplina es la base fundamental del funcionamiento de la Guardia Nacional; su\nobjeto es el fiel y exacto cumplimiento de los deberes que prescriben las leyes y reglamentos\naplicables y se sustenta en la obediencia, el honor, la justicia y la ética.\nArtículo 62. El personal de la Guardia Nacional que tenga mando deberá comunicar a sus\n\nsubordinados la importancia de cumplir con las leyes, reglamentos y órdenes emanadas de la\nsuperioridad. Su actuación se regirá inspirada en el cumplimiento del deber, por encima de otro\ninterés o consideración personal; en consecuencia, no permitirá que se propalen\nmurmuraciones, quejas o descontentos que impidan el cumplimiento de las obligaciones o que\ndepriman el ánimo de sus subordinados.\nArtículo 63. El personal de la Guardia Nacional que infrinja uno de los deberes previstos en esta\nLey, se hará acreedor a alguno de los correctivos disciplinarios y sanciones siguientes:\nI. Amonestación;\nII. Arresto;\nIII. Restricción;\nIV. Suspensión de empleo;\nV. Cambio de unidad, dependencia, instalación o comisión en observación de su conducta, y\nVI. Remoción.\nPara efectos de este Capítulo el arresto consiste en el confinamiento en espacios especiales y\ntendrá una duración máxima de treinta y seis horas; en el caso de los Comisarios, será de\nhasta veinticuatro horas.\nLa restricción consiste en la obligación de permanecer a disposición de su superior jerárquico,\nsin poder disponer de su tiempo libre. La persona sancionada no podrá salir de las\ninstalaciones de su adscripción o comisión, salvo en actividades propias de sus funciones que\nle ordene un superior jerárquico.\nLa restricción tendrá una duración máxima de quince días.\nArtículo 64. Quien amoneste lo hará de manera que ninguna persona de menor jerarquía a la\ndel amonestado conozca de la aplicación de la medida y observará la discreción que exige la\ndisciplina. Queda prohibida la reprensión.\nArtículo 65. El arresto y la restricción se impondrán de conformidad con las reglas siguientes:\n\nI. Pueden imponer los correctivos disciplinarios a los subordinados, los superiores jerárquicos o\nde cargo;\nII. Tienen facultad para graduar los correctivos disciplinarios:\na) El Comandante, y\nb) Los Coordinadores Territoriales, Estatales, de Unidad de nivel Batallón, y los jefes y oficiales\ncomandantes de destacamento.\nEn ausencia de los anteriores, la facultad recaerá en quien los suceda en el mando o cargo;\nIII. Quien imponga el correctivo disciplinario dará cuenta a la autoridad competente para su\ngraduación, siendo esta quien fijará su duración, teniendo en consideración la jerarquía de\nquien lo impuso, la falta cometida y los antecedentes del subordinado;\nIV. Toda orden de arresto o restricción deberá darse por escrito. En caso de que un integrante\nde la Guardia Nacional se vea precisado a darla de manera verbal, surtirá efectos de inmediato,\npero dicha medida deberá ser ratificada por escrito dentro de las veinticuatro horas siguientes,\nde manera fundada y motivada. En caso de que no se ratifique, la orden quedará sin efecto, y\nV. Quien impida el cumplimiento de un arresto o restricción, el que permita que se quebranten,\nasí como el que no los cumpla, será sancionado.\nArtículo 66. La restricción se impondrá de acuerdo a lo siguiente:\nI. A los Comisarios hasta por treinta y seis horas;\nII. A los Inspectores Jefe y a los Inspectores, hasta por cuarenta y ocho horas;\nIII. A los Subinspectores, Oficiales y Suboficiales, hasta por ocho días, y\nIV. Al resto del personal de la Guardia Nacional, hasta por quince días.\nEl personal que no tenga destino fijo y se encuentre en disponibilidad, cumplirá la restricción en\ncualquiera de los recintos de la Guardia Nacional.\nEl Comandante podrá imponer, en todos los casos, restricción hasta por quince días.\nArtículo 67. El personal de la Guardia Nacional facultado para graduar arrestos y restricción\n\ntendrá en cuenta al hacerlo que éstos sean proporcionales a la falta cometida, a la jerarquía, al\ncargo, a los antecedentes del infractor, a las circunstancias, al grado que ostente y al cargo de\nquien lo impuso.\nEl personal de la Guardia Nacional facultado para graduar arrestos y restricciones podrá\ndejarlos sin efecto o sustituirlos por amonestación.\nArtículo 68. El personal de la Guardia Nacional que esté cumpliendo un arresto o restricción y\nse haga acreedor a otro, empezará a cumplir este último desde el momento en que se le\ncomunique.\nArtículo 69. El personal de la Guardia Nacional que haya recibido orden de arresto o restricción\ndeberá comunicar al superior de quien dependa, así como al que se la impuso, el inicio y\ntérmino de su cumplimiento.\nCapítulo II\nDe los Consejos de Disciplina\nArtículo 70. Los Consejos de Disciplina funcionarán con carácter permanente y sus\nresoluciones serán autónomas; por cuanto hace a su organización, integración, funcionamiento\ny procedimiento administrativo, se sujetarán a lo dispuesto en las disposiciones legales\naplicables y en el Reglamento.\nArtículo 71. Los Consejos de Disciplina son competentes para conocer, resolver y sancionar las\nfaltas en contra de la disciplina cometidas por el personal de la Guardia Nacional, así como\ncalificar la conducta o actuación del citado personal, y serán:\nI. El Consejo de Comisarios, que conocerá de las faltas que cometan los Comisarios en\ncualquier situación en que se encuentren, los Inspectores con mando y los miembros del\nConsejo de Honor Superior. El Consejo de Comisarios funcionará en la sede del Secretario;\nII. El Consejo de Honor Superior, que conocerá de las faltas que cometan los Inspectores sin\nmando, en cualquier situación en que se encuentren, así como aquellas en las que incurran los\n\nOficiales con mando y los integrantes de los Consejos de Honor Ordinario. El Consejo de Honor\nSuperior funcionará en la sede de la Comandancia, y\nIII. Los Consejos de Honor Ordinario, que conocerán de las faltas que cometan los Oficiales sin\nmando y el personal de Escala Básica; estos Consejos funcionarán en la sede de las\nCoordinaciones Territoriales, Estatales y de Unidad.\nVI.5.1\n. No estamos ante un caso de omisión legislativa.\n506.\nDel régimen constitucional de la Guardia Nacional, incluyendo el sus artículos transitorios y el proceso\nlegislativo correspondiente, no es posible desprender la existencia de un mandato directo para legislar,\nde forma particular, un procedimiento para ejercer el derecho de defensa en contra de las correcciones\ndisciplinarias.\n507.\nEn efecto, para considerar que existe una omisión legislativa de ejercicio obligatorio es necesario que el\nórgano legislativo emita una ley teniendo una obligación o un mandato para hacerlo, pero lo realice de\nmanera incompleta o deficiente\n(223)\ny en los artículos transitorios de la reforma constitucional en\nmateria de Guardia Nacional no se advierte mandato constitucional para el legislador ordinario para\nestablecer en específico reglas del derecho de audiencia en los procedimientos administrativos de\ndisciplina a los miembros de esa Guardia.\n508.\nLo que se desprende del texto de ese régimen transitorio:\n[\n...\n\n] La Ley de la Guardia Nacional contendrá, al menos, los siguientes elementos\n:\n1.\nLos supuestos para la coordinación y colaboración de la Guardia Nacional con las\ninstituciones de seguridad pública de las entidades federativas y de los Municipios;\n2.\nLas reglas para determinar las aportaciones de las entidades federativas y Municipios\ncuando soliciten la colaboración de la Guardia Nacional para la atención de tareas de seguridad\npública de competencia local;\n3.\nLo relativo a la estructura jerárquica, regímenes de disciplina que incluya faltas, delitos y\nsanciones a la disciplina policial, responsabilidades y servicios, ascensos, prestaciones,\ningreso, educación, capacitación, profesionalización y el cumplimiento de las responsabilidades\ny tareas que puedan homologarse, en lo conducente a las disposiciones aplicables en el ámbito\nde la Fuerza Armada permanente;\n4.\nLos criterios de evaluación del desempeño de sus integrantes;\n5.\nLa regulación sobre la disposición, posesión, portación y uso de armas de fuego, atendiendo\nlos estándares y mejores prácticas internacionales;\n6.\nLas hipótesis para la delimitación de la actuación de sus integrantes;\n7.\nLos requisitos que deberán cumplir sus integrantes, conforme a las leyes aplicables, y\n8.\n\nLos componentes mínimos del informe anual a que se refiere la fracción IV del artículo 76 de\nesta Constitución.\nIII.\nLa Ley Nacional sobre el Uso de la Fuerza establecerá, por lo menos, las siguientes\nprevisiones:\n[\n...\n]\nIV.\nLa Ley Nacional del Registro de Detenciones incorporará, al menos, las siguientes\nprevisiones:\n[\n...\n]\n509.\nEstas consideraciones son vinculantes, al haberse aprobado por mayoría de ocho votos de las señoras\nMinistras y de los señores Ministros Gutiérrez Ortiz Mena, Esquivel Mossa, Ortiz Ahlf, Zaldívar Lelo de\nLarrea, Ríos Farjat, Laynez Potisek, Pérez Dayán y Presidenta Piña Hernández. Los señores Ministros\nGonzález Alcántara Carrancá, Aguilar Morales y Pardo Rebolledo votaron en contra.\nVI.5.2\n. Régimen disciplinario, incluyendo el derecho de audiencia.\n510.\nEs importante precisar que los artículos que la Comisión impugna pertenecen al Régimen Disciplinario\nde la Ley de la Guardia Nacional que se refiere a las responsabilidades y procedimientos sancionatorios.\nAsí, del análisis de las normas impugnadas se obtiene que el derecho de audiencia se encuentra en ese\n\ncapítulo de forma expresa y por remisión al reglamento de esa ley, para los casos de disciplina que\ndeben guardar los miembros de ese cuerpo policial.\n511.\nEn efecto, en el artículo 57\n(224)\nimpugnado se indica que el personal de la Guardia Nacional deberá de\nsujetar su conducta a la observancia de la leyes, órdenes y jerarquías, a la obediencia a la superioridad,\nasí como al Código de Ética de la Guardia Nacional. Asimismo, en el precepto 59\n(225)\nimpugnado se\nseñala que el personal de la Guardia Nacional que infrinja su ley o su reglamento será acreedor de una\ncorrectivo o sanción disciplinaria, pues en el numeral 61\n(226)\ncontrovertido se precisa que la disciplina\nes la base fundamental del funcionamiento de la Guardia Nacional.\n512.\nDe ese contenido se puede concluir que las normas controvertidas se enmarcan en la disciplina que\ncorresponde y es necesaria en los miembros de este tipo de instituciones.\n513.\nEs necesario destacar que el régimen de disciplina policiaca contribuye a fortalecer a la institución de la\nGuardia Nacional y sus sanciones aportan al debido funcionamiento y ejercicio de las facultades de las\npersonas que forman parte de ésta en cargos policiales, por ello el derecho de audiencia para algunas\ncorrecciones disciplinarias puede no funcionar exactamente como el de cualquier otro procedimiento, ya\nque los correctivos y las sanciones que corresponden a esa disciplina deben tener un efecto que\ngarantice el cumplimiento de los deberes como institución y la posibilidad de generar ejemplo en el resto\n\ndel cuerpo\n(227)\n.\n514.\nEsas necesidades de efectividad inmediata y de ejemplo que tienen ciertas medidas disciplinarias en un\ncuerpo policiaco llevan a que el derecho de audiencia en este campo no corresponda a su ejercicio en\notro tipo de procedimientos, ya que la rapidez o celeridad en el correctivo disciplinario hace que sea\nadecuado que se reclame su imposición de un modo diferente y, quizás hasta posterior, lo que impide\nque se cuestione de manera innecesaria la autoridad y el criterio que dio lugar a la sanción disciplinaria,\ncon que se beneficia a la disciplina de la institución policiaca, por la rapidez de la medida que disciplina a\nuna persona concreta y que permea como ejemplo al grupo de policías, lo que impacta en el desempeño\nde sus facultades\n(228)\n.\n515.\nConforme a las anteriores consideraciones este Pleno encuentra que el régimen disciplinario de la\nGuardia Nacional, como institución policiaca, contiene una norma que contempla el derecho de\naudiencia, esto es, el artículo 58\n(229)\ncontrovertido, ya que en éste se establece que el personal que\ntenga alguna queja en relación con las órdenes de sus superiores o las obligaciones que les imponga el\nservicio, podrá acudir ante el superior inmediato para la solución de sus demandas y, en caso de no ser\ndebidamente atendido, podrá recurrir en rigurosa escala hasta el Comandante si es necesario.\n516.\nAdemás, es importante subrayar que en el artículo 65, fracción IV, controvertido se indica que algunos\n\ncorrectivos disciplinarios deben darse por escrito y que de hacerse de manera verbal deberán ratificarse\npor escrito en las veinticuatro horas siguientes, de manera fundada y motivada y que sin la ratificación la\nmedida no surtirá efecto\n(230)\n. El contenido precisado abunda en el ejercicio del derecho de defensa al\nconocer las razones de la medida correctiva que podrá controvertirse, pues algunas decisiones\ncorrectivas para su imposición deben atender a su proporcionalidad, en relación con la falta cometida, la\njerarquía, al cargo, a los antecedentes del infractor, a las circunstancias al grado que se ostente y al\ngrado de quien la impuso, de acuerdo con el artículo 67 controvertido.\n517.\nAsimismo, en el artículo 70 controvertido se indica que los Consejos de Disciplina en cuanto al\nprocedimiento administrativo se sujetarán a lo dispuesto en las disposiciones legales aplicables y al\nReglamento. En este último\n(231)\nse regula el régimen disciplinario\n(232)\ny se indica que en las\ncuestiones disciplinarias rige el derecho a la presunción de inocencia\n(233)\n. Y se establece un capítulo\nque regula la comparecencia de las personas sometidas a un procedimiento disciplinario, en el que se\nindica que se notificará por escrito la obligación de presentarse ante el Consejo de Disciplina, el derecho\npara nombrar defensor\n(234)\n, se precisa que existirá una audiencia pública\n\n(235)\nen la que la persona\npresunta infractora puede ejercer su derecho de defensa mediante un defensor y en la que está\nhabilitado para ofrecer pruebas, presentar alegatos y formular conclusiones.\n518.\nA lo anterior se suma que en ese reglamento también se establece un sistema de excusas y\nrecusaciones\n(236)\n, así como un recurso de inconformidad\n(237)\ny en cuanto a lo relacionado con el\nprocedimiento ordinario de esos consejos, la substanciación del recurso y todo aquello que no esté\nregulado por el mismo, se atenderá supletoriamente a la Ley Federal de Procedimiento Administrativo y\nel Código Federal de Procedimientos Civiles\n(238)\n.\n519.\nEn consecuencia, sí existe la regulación del derecho de audiencia en la Ley de la Guardia y su\nreglamento, de acuerdo con lo expuesto en los párrafos anteriores.\n520.\nAdemás, la regulación de ese procedimiento en el reglamento referido en el caso no atenta contra los\nderechos de las personas de la corporación policiaca, pues conforme a l\na jurisprudencia P./J. 30/2007,\nemitida por este Tribunal Pleno, de rubro\n\"", "excerpt_sha256": "828316b26ebcd5658c141cab50ade474ec492364efced2b6980cbb72f77e0ea8", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}]
